 Abstract

 We introduce AlphaProof Code, a multi-agent AI framework for formal software specification and verification that we used to prove key security properties of libsignal, the Signal protocol’s reference implementation, at the machine-code level. AlphaProof Code consists of a human-guided agent for writing Lean specifications of code, a formal semantics of AArch64 machine code, and a team of autonomous prover agents whose proofs are accepted only if Lean’s kernel checks them against the approved specification. Applied to libsignal, our agents generated about 6.5 million lines of machine-checked Lean proof code, resting on about 26,000 lines of trusted specifications and AArch64 semantics. The proofs cover Signal’s post-quantum PQXDH handshake and Triple Ratchet, establishing forward secrecy, authentication, post-compromise security, and handshake secrecy. They are the first proofs connecting protocol-level security guarantees to the unmodified code of a widely used cryptographic protocol implementation. The results suggest that AI can make formal verification, historically too expensive for most software, practical for real-world systems. Our Lean development is available at https://google-deepmind.github.io/code-verification [https://google-deepmind.github.io/code-verification].

 1 Introduction

 Ensuring that the software that powers our world is correct and secure is one of the longest-standing challenges in computing [98 [#bib-turing1949checking]]. Today, assurance primarily comes from testing, bug finding, and code review. However, reactive bug finding has inherent limitations, even when applying advanced AI systems for cyber-defense [29 [#bib-projectzero2024bigsleep], 10 [#bib-anthropic2026mythos], 77 [#bib-openai2025aardvark]] to find and fix bugs quickly. An alternative is formal verification [54 [#bib-floyd1967assigning], 60 [#bib-hoare1969axiomatic], 61 [#bib-hoare2003verifying]], where one specifies the correctness and security properties that a software system must enforce and then mathematically proves that the system satisfies them. Such a proof rules out every bug or attack that would violate those properties.

 For all of its potential benefits, formal verification is notoriously difficult. While there have been heroic efforts to verify important, complex systems, they come at a high cost. The formally verified seL4 microkernel—perhaps the biggest success story in the field—took twenty person-years to verify [66 [#bib-klein2009sel4]]; the CompCert verified C compiler [73 [#bib-leroy2009compcert]] took six. Further, in these successful examples, the source code of the system was written specifically with verification in mind. Verification of pre-existing industrial-strength code, such as a production library as compiled by an optimizing compiler, is even harder, as the prover cannot modify the code to make the proof simpler. Such labor costs are untenable for all but the most mission-critical domains.

 In this paper, we show that recent AI advances can drastically reduce these costs. We present AlphaProof Code (Figure 1 [#fig-workflow]), a framework in which a human user and AI agents collaboratively write Lean [45 [#bib-moura2021lean4]] specifications of real-world code, and, once the user has vetted a specification, a team of autonomous agents proves that the code satisfies it. We used the framework to verify key security properties of libsignal, the reference implementation of the Signal protocol [91 [#bib-libsignal], 74 [#bib-marlinspike2013ratcheting]], at the machine-code level. Our results are a significant milestone towards a grand challenge launched in April 2026 [23 [#bib-signalshot]], and achieving them would have required years of expert labor with earlier tools.

 Signal (§3 [#sec-signal]) is the de facto global standard for end-to-end encrypted messaging. It protects the communications of billions of people worldwide: in addition to the standalone Signal application, variants of the protocol are deployed in WhatsApp, Meta’s Messenger app, and Google Messages. The protocol includes sophisticated components such as the PQXDH handshake [69 [#bib-pqxdh-spec]] and the Triple Ratchet [48 [#bib-dodis2025triple]], which are designed to ensure the security of messages even when some of the keys are compromised, and even against future adversaries with access to a quantum computer.

 There is a long line of work on formal security verification of cryptographic protocols [49 [#bib-dolev1983security], 31 [#bib-blanchet2008cryptoverif], 17 [#bib-barthe2011easycrypt], 86 [#bib-schmidt2012dh], 15 [#bib-arquint2023sound]], including Signal [43 [#bib-cohngordon2020signal], 28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh], 52 [#bib-fg24], 40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet]]. However, all of these efforts target abstract protocol models, verification-friendly reimplementations, implementations modified to simplify verification, or narrower properties such as crash-freedom (§8 [#sec-related]). By contrast, we give the first proofs of protocol-level security properties for a widely deployed, unmodified implementation of a cryptographic protocol. More generally, our work shows that AI can verify machine code against rich specifications from the research literature, including properties of the interaction between a stateful program and an adaptive adversary.

 [paper/figures/fig-workflow.svg]

Figure 1. The AlphaProof Code workflow. (1) A specification-writer agent works with the user to turn models and properties from the literature into a Lean specification. Reviewer agents critique each draft, and the user approves the result. (2) The unmodified Rust code is compiled with the standard compiler, and the executable is loaded into Lean together with a semantics of AArch64. (3) A team of prover agents proves the specification about the executable. If the specification is vacuous or otherwise incorrect, the provers can propose amendments, which are approved based on the user-set policy or explicit judgment. A gatekeeper agent accepts a proof only if Lean’s kernel checks it and the Lean comparator confirms that it proves exactly a held-out copy of the specification. Shaded items are the ones a reader must trust. In addition, the results trust Lean’s kernel, the security of the cryptographic primitives (stated as assumptions in the computational proof and idealized in the symbolic proof), and the contracts of some routines that the executable calls (§4 [#sec-interface]).

 We link libsignal into a small command-line harness and compile it with the stock rustc compiler to AArch64 Linux machine code, with low-level cryptographic primitives specified by contracts (§4 [#sec-interface]). We use AlphaProof Code to verify the code’s security in the two standard models of protocol security: the symbolic model (aka the Dolev–Yao model) [49 [#bib-dolev1983security]], which treats those cryptographic primitives as abstract “black boxes” achieving standard security properties, and the computational model [56 [#bib-goldwasser1984probabilistic]], which bounds an efficient attacker’s success probability by the hardness of the underlying primitives.

 Our specification-writing agents start with established symbolic [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet], 28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]] and computational [7 [#bib-alwen2019doubleratchet], 52 [#bib-fg24]] specifications for Signal and adapt them to Lean. However, the existing specifications do not take us all the way, as they analyze the handshake and the ratchet separately and predate Signal’s post-quantum ratchet. With human guidance, our agents extend these specifications to the protocol as deployed. Our agent-written proofs of these specifications establish forward secrecy, post-compromise security, and message confidentiality and authenticity directly for the compiled binary (Table 1 [#tab-what-we-prove], §5.1 [#sec-sym], §5.2 [#sec-comp]), against classical attackers, and, in the symbolic model, handshake secrecy against a future quantum attacker.

 Our proofs amount to about 6.5 million lines of Lean. The statements and definitions a reader must read and trust are about 18,000 lines of code in our repository, plus about 8,000 lines of the AArch64 semantics and the symbol names in a Lean representation of the compiled machine code, whose bytes Lean checks against the executable. To check that our specifications catch real mistakes, we re-introduced two previously fixed libsignal bugs [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet]] and proved in Lean that the resulting binaries violate our symbolic security theorems (§6 [#sec-bug-injection]). Our Lean development, with all specifications and proofs, is available at the website https://google-deepmind.github.io/code-verification [https://google-deepmind.github.io/code-verification].

 2 AlphaProof Code

 AlphaProof Code (Figure 1 [#fig-workflow]) is a framework for human–AI collaboration on the formal specification and verification of real-world software. The framework is based on a simple principle: The majority of human effort in software verification now lies in engineering the specification, the formal statement that is proved and its assumptions about how the machine code executes. Previously, the expertise and labor required to write proofs was a bottleneck to large-scale formal verification, but this is no longer the case. As we demonstrate, even large proofs about software can be written by teams of AI agents once a specification is fixed, and the proof agents can work autonomously: since the proof is ultimately checked by the Lean kernel, the reliability of the results does not depend on the reliability of these proving agents.

 For large, complex systems, AI assistance can reduce the cost of specification writing and assurance but not completely replace human judgment and review. Based on these observations, we propose that human authors select what to prove, resolve ambiguities, evaluate security trade-offs, and make all final decisions, while AI agents draft and test specifications under human direction.

 We now describe the four components of AlphaProof Code (Figure 1 [#fig-workflow]) in turn.

 2.1 Specification Writing

 A specification in AlphaProof Code consists of a set of Lean theorem statements together with every definition they depend on, such as models of the code’s environment. The specification defines what correctness and security mean for the program, and what classes of bugs and attacks the proof excludes. The specification must be trusted, meaning that the validity of the final result depends on getting it right, and it cannot be automatically checked—a flaw could weaken the guarantee the theorem provides (shaded in Figure 1 [#fig-workflow]). By contrast, the multi-million-line proofs produced by AI agents are untrusted: any logical error in a proof is rejected by Lean’s kernel, preventing the proof from going through. The trusted definitions and theorem statements in the specification build on knowledge available in research papers and standards. The definitions can also include extern contracts that define assumptions about parts of the system that are outside the scope of the verification campaign. For the Signal proofs, the trusted computing base is described in §5.3 [#sec-tcb].

 All agents in AlphaProof Code, including the specification writer, are general-purpose coding agents whose behavior is defined by skills: natural-language documents that describe an agent’s task, the sources it should consult and the checks it must perform. The specification-writer agent drafts trusted theorems and definitions under close guidance from the user. A pool of reviewer agents checks that a draft is faithful to its sources and to the implementation.

 Even when a model from the literature exists, it can take many rounds of iteration between the user and the agents to arrive at a good specification. For instance, prior formal models of Signal analyzed its initial handshake and its ratchet separately. Our specifications had to compose these models and cover Signal’s new post-quantum ratchet (§5.1 [#sec-sym], §5.2 [#sec-comp]), and this took several rounds of drafts and review. The user reviews, revises, and approves the specification before any proofs are written, and its git commit becomes the baseline that the gatekeeper later checks proofs against.

 Much of this iteration is designed to ensure the specification’s strength. A theorem that makes overly restrictive assumptions on the inputs, asserts only weak properties of the output, or places too few constraints on existentially quantified witnesses can hold even for buggy code. For instance, the computational security proof states that there exists a reduction from an attack on the protocol to a violation on an underlying assumption (§5.2.2 [#sec-reductions]). If a mistake in the specification weakens the capability of the attacker, then the security guarantee is also weakened. This is the sort of mistake that the iteration is designed to prevent.

 For this reason, we use several techniques to test the specifications and assure their quality. Human authors review the specification; even so, specifications are like any large software artifact, in that human review is not expected to detect all bugs in a large codebase. Therefore, AlphaProof Code also includes several means of automated testing. First, reviewer agents are asked to provide reviews from a skeptical perspective, and in our experience were effective at detecting soundness and strength issues in the specification. Second, the prover agents create theorems to check the specification’s quality, which are formally proved. For example, contradictory hypotheses make a theorem vacuous, so the agents also prove that the hypotheses are jointly satisfiable and that the theorems’ premises are met by concrete executions, in the spirit of vacuity detection in model checking [20 [#bib-beer1997vacuity], 70 [#bib-kupferman2003vacuity]]. Third, mutation testing in software engineering is a well-known technique for evaluating the effectiveness of test suites by injecting artificial mistakes into the code and seeing if the test suite fails [47 [#bib-demillo1978hints], 64 [#bib-jia2011analysis]]. To test specifications this way, agents add natural bugs to the code, such as skipping the ciphertext MAC verification in Signal, and prove that the security theorem is false when applied to this mutated binary. Finally, we check that the specifications would have prohibited historical bugs in the system (§6 [#sec-bug-injection]), again in the sense that the symbolic security theorem is provably false.

 2.2 Machine-Code Semantics

 Our theorems are stated over a formal semantics of user-mode AArch64 Linux, written in Lean. The semantics takes an executable (an ELF binary) as a list of bytes and describes all of its possible runs as a relation between inputs, such as command-line arguments, standard input and the initial file-system state, and outcomes, such as the exit code, standard output and the final file-system state. The central definition, ElfRunsTo [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/arm64-semantics/Arm64/Trusted/Process/Semantics.lean#L126] bin pre post, states that every run of bin from inputs satisfying pre ends in an outcome satisfying post, and that no run faults, for instance by reading unmapped memory. For the theorems to apply to real executions, the semantics must be sound: every behavior of the real hardware and kernel must be a possible run of the semantics. This cannot be proved, since the hardware and kernel are not formal objects. Consequently, our approach is to keep the trusted definitions small and test them extensively.

 Lean definition: ElfRunsTo

 The semantics models a single-threaded, statically linked process. A machine state holds the general-purpose and SIMD/floating-point registers, the program counter, the condition flags, a byte-addressed memory with per-page permissions, and the files in the current directory. A decoder turns the bytes at the program counter into an instruction, and the effect of each instruction follows Arm’s machine-readable ASL specification [12 [#bib-arm2026a64isa], 13 [#bib-arm2026arm], 84 [#bib-reid2016trustworthy]]. Because code and data share one memory, writes to executable memory, accesses to unmapped memory and permission violations are faults, so a proof that a run terminates normally rules them out. Above the instruction level, a loader parses the ELF file, maps its segments and builds the initial stack, as Linux does when it starts a process. System calls are interpreted by a model of the calls the program uses: memory management, file and stream I/O, and exit. A run ends in one of two ways: it exits with an exit code, or it exits abnormally and faults due to an error, a panic, or an unmodeled instruction or system call. The semantics covers 467 instructions and 31 system calls in 7,578 lines of Lean. It does not model threads, signals, the vDSO, dynamic linking, or files outside the current directory, so programs that rely on them cannot be verified.

 The trusted parts of the semantics, namely the decoder, instruction semantics, memory model, loader and system-call model, contain only definitions. The program logic used in the proofs is not trusted: its separation-logic rules, weakest-precondition calculus and proof automation are all proved sound in Lean against ElfRunsTo [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/arm64-semantics/Arm64/Trusted/Process/Semantics.lean#L126]. We validate the trusted definitions by intensive testing. Instruction execution is tested in lockstep against two independent oracles: the Unicorn emulator (whose code is derived from QEMU), and the Sail model of Armv9.4-A [14 [#bib-armstrong2019sail]], derived from Arm’s official specification. The tests include single instructions, published test vectors, random instruction sequences and compiled Rust functions. The loader and the system-call model are tested against qemu-aarch64 on a large number of binaries.

 Two features of the semantics matter for specifications. First, the semantics treats the physical memory available to the process as a finite input, and a run that exhausts it ends in an out-of-memory outcome. A theorem then either proves, given a lower bound on available memory, that no run exhausts it, or accounts explicitly for the runs that do (§5.2.4 [#sec-assm]). It is assumed that the operating system provides physical memory if available. Second, a proof can hook a function (linked either from a library or as a four-byte trap stub in the executable): a call to it is intercepted at entry and modeled with a trusted contract instead of executing code. This is how assumptions about cryptographic primitives enter a result, since their security cannot itself be proved, and it is also used for error-message formatting and a few routines such as the functions that write files and standard output. Hooks are part of the specification and are reviewed carefully (§2.1 [#sec-spec]).

 2.3 Proof Synthesis

 Our proofs are produced autonomously by a team of agents with distinct roles, each defined by a skill. An orchestrator divides up the work and schedules agents but never edits Lean files itself. Each subtask is assigned to a proof worker. The main difficulty in a large team is managing context: each agent’s context window is limited, and state can be lost when the context is compacted. The orchestrator therefore groups workers under team leads, each responsible for a larger, longer-running goal. A lead absorbs the back-and-forth with its workers and sends the orchestrator short summaries of open obligations, completed lemmas and blockers. Agents also try to falsify a goal before work on it starts, to avoid wasting resources on unprovable subgoals.

 A few fixed roles keep the shared codebase coherent as the proofs grow. An architect designs, and periodically revises, the high-level decomposition of the proof. An integrator merges verified proofs into library modules, removes duplicate lemmas and generalizes lemmas for reuse. To avoid conflicts among dozens of concurrent agents, each file is edited by at most one worker at a time, and only the integrator commits to the repository. Other support roles schedule expensive Lean builds and monitor agents and resources. In our proof campaigns, a team typically had 40 to 80 agents and ran on a single machine for 24 to 72 hours.

 Proof attempts can reveal that a specification is subtly wrong about the code or its environment. Agents cannot edit the specification, but they can propose an amendment to a critic agent, which never sees the proof attempts. The critic accepts an amendment only if three conditions hold. First, the amendment must be necessary, as shown by a machine-checked disproof, a counterexample on the binary or an argument grounded in the code. Second, it must not weaken security, and any assumption it adds must hold of the real code. Third, it must be small, typically a few lines in one definition. Amendments that change trusted assumptions are approved by the user or under a user-set policy, and each is logged with its justification. Examples from our campaign include amendments that strengthen trusted contracts for library functions to match their real behavior, for example, “memcpy may copy from read-only memory.”

 2.4 Gatekeeping

 A proof is accepted only if it proves exactly the statements the user approved. Inspecting the theorem statement alone is not enough to check this, since a statement can be weakened indirectly by changing a definition it depends on. The gatekeeper is an agent used during development to check recorded approvals for specification amendments (if any) and to call the standard comparator tool [72 [#bib-comparator]] to check the proof.

 The agent invokes comparator using a deterministic script that can be rerun manually when desired. We keep a strict discipline of which files are in the trusted specification of the theorem versus the untrusted proof, enforced by deterministic scripts, which allows clean rerunning of comparator. At the start of a campaign, the gatekeeper copies the specification from the baseline commit into a held-out directory that no agent can write to. The held-out copy changes only through accepted amendments (§2.3 [#sec-proof]), which are reviewed by the critic and approved by human authors before the gatekeeper applies them.

 The gatekeeper accepts a proof only if comparator confirms that the statement and every definition it depends on are identical to this copy, that the proof uses only Lean’s standard axioms, and that Lean’s kernel accepts the proof. At that point, the gatekeeper declares that the proof is complete (proof agents, team leads, and the orchestrator cannot declare a top-level goal done on their own). When proof development is complete, we re-run comparator manually for further assurance.

 3 Background on Signal

 [paper/figures/fig-signal.svg]

Figure 2. A Signal session as implemented by libsignal [74 [#bib-marlinspike2013ratcheting], 91 [#bib-libsignal]]. Bob uploads his prekey bundle to the server; Alice fetches it, completes the handshake (PQXDH) on her own, and sends a prekey message that carries the handshake values together with her first encrypted message. She keeps sending prekey messages until Bob replies; after that, the parties exchange ordinary Triple Ratchet messages, each under a fresh key. Figure 8 [#fig-signal-detail] in Appendix A [#app-signal] adds the notation and the key schedule.

 Signal is a protocol for asynchronous messaging, meaning that it allows a user Alice to start a conversation with a user Bob and send him messages while he is offline. A server stores and relays messages but is not trusted for either confidentiality or authenticity. We describe the version implemented by libsignal v0.105.4, restricted to one-to-one sessions. (We leave group messaging as future work.) A session has two phases (Figure 2 [#fig-signal]): a handshake, in which Alice and Bob agree on a shared secret, and a ratchet, which derives a fresh key from that secret for every message.

 The handshake relies on Diffie-Hellman (DH) key agreement: Alice combines her private key with Bob’s public key, Bob does the reverse, and both get the same value, which an eavesdropper cannot compute from the public keys alone. Since Bob may be offline, he leaves public keys, called prekeys, on the server for senders to use. Signal’s handshake, PQXDH [69 [#bib-pqxdh-spec]], mixes several DH values with a post-quantum key encapsulation mechanism (KEM). The resulting secret survives the leak of some of these keys, and an eavesdropper who records the handshake cannot recover it even with a future quantum computer.

 The ratchet, which Signal calls the Triple Ratchet [48 [#bib-dodis2025triple]], combines the classical Double Ratchet with the Sparse Post-Quantum Ratchet (SPQR) [89 [#bib-spqr]]. This design contains the damage from a stolen key. Each message key is derived from the previous state by a one-way function and then deleted; stealing a party’s current state therefore exposes no earlier encrypted messages (forward secrecy). Every reply also mixes in a fresh DH value, which locks out an adversary who stole the state, as long as it stays passive during that fresh DH exchange (post-compromise security).

 Our goal is to show that these properties hold against a strong adversary that controls the network and the server and may compromise long-term keys, prekeys or a party’s entire state. For the post-quantum properties, the adversary may later break DH. §5.1 [#sec-sym] and §5.2 [#sec-comp] state secrecy, authentication, forward secrecy and post-compromise security precisely.

 The subsequent sections of this paper refer to a few more of libsignal’s components. Bob’s prekey bundle holds his long-term X25519 identity key, a signed prekey and a Kyber1024 prekey,1 [#fn-1] both signed with the identity key using XEdDSA, and optionally a one-time prekey that the server hands out only once. On the ratchet side, a root key absorbs the fresh DH values and yields chain keys, from which message keys are derived. SPQR runs a separate ML-KEM-768 exchange and mixes its keys into every message key, so that post-compromise security also holds against a quantum adversary. Appendix A [#app-signal] has the full key schedule.

Table 1. Summary of our verification results. We verify libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179], an AArch64 executable built by stock rustc from the unmodified libsignal v0.105.4 and SPQR crates. Cryptographic primitives, the two output functions and a few library routines are specified by contracts (§4 [#sec-interface]); everything else is verified. Besides the specifications, the proofs require trust in Lean’s kernel and the AArch64 semantics, though not the Rust source or compiler.

Symbolic model (§5.1 [#sec-sym])Computational model (§5.2 [#sec-comp])

AttackerControls the network and can compromise long-term keys, prekeys and session state. Can also break DH (modeling a quantum attacker), or the KEM (modeling cryptanalysis). Unbounded, but limited to symbolic operations on messages.Controls the network and can compromise long-term keys, prekeys and session state, except at a few points where compromise would make winning trivial. Any classical algorithm whose running time and queries are bounded.

GuaranteeTen theorems, each holding on every collision-free execution (Table 2 [#tab-theorems]): forward secrecy and post-compromise security of message keys, handshake authentication and KCI resistance, and handshake secrecy, even if a quantum attacker later breaks DH. No run faults or diverges.Confidentiality and authenticity of messages, with forward secrecy and post-compromise security, for PQXDH composed with the Triple Ratchet, as a concrete bound on the attacker’s advantage (Theorem 2 [#thm-main]).

AssumptionsIdealized primitives: hashes, KDFs and MACs never collide and cannot be inverted, secrets never repeat, and ciphertexts (resp., signatures) cannot be decrypted (resp., forged) without the key. These are idealized forms of standard security properties that real primitives are believed to have, except with negligible probability.Gap-DH and XEdDSA unforgeability, Kyber1024 collision resistance, PRF/PRG security of HKDF and HMAC, and one-time CCA security of AES-CBC with HMAC (§5.2.4 [#sec-assm]).

Trusted modelThe symbolic model and the event layer that maps runs of the binary to symbolic-model events.The formalized security definitions and assumptions, and the byte-level Lean model of libsignal (§5.3 [#sec-tcb]).

Not coveredComputational secrecy, more than two parties, and SPQR’s post-quantum healing (§5.1.6 [#sec-sym-limitations]).Quantum attackers, multi-device state, adversarial first messages to sessions that are later promoted, and large scale: the bound becomes vacuous for multiple long sessions with many epochs (§5.2.5 [#sec-limitations]).

 The code does much more than the protocol described above, and this is where implementation bugs arise. For example, libsignal keeps a large mutable session state, stores keys for messages that arrive out of order, splits SPQR’s ML-KEM messages into chunks spread over ordinary messages, and uses protobuf for serialization. It also keeps archived earlier sessions so that delayed messages can still be decrypted. Because our theorems are about the compiled code, they cover this machinery. In one now-fixed bug, a prekey message that decrypted only under an archived session caused libsignal to store the new session’s state anyway, including a copy of the signed prekey’s secret. When we reintroduce it, the resulting binary provably violates our symbolic security theorem (§6 [#sec-bug-injection]).

 Table 1 [#tab-what-we-prove] summarizes our verification results for the protocol. More details are available in §5 [#sec-verification] and the appendix.

 1 Kyber1024 is the round-3 predecessor of ML-KEM-1024 (FIPS 203); libsignal’s PQXDH still uses it. ↩ [#fnref-1]

 4 Setup for Verification

 We verify the one-to-one session code of libsignal-protocol (v0.105.4, with spqr v1.6.0): PQXDH session setup and Triple Ratchet messaging. Rather than reasoning about its Rust functions and in-memory state, we wrap the unmodified crates in a small command-line harness, libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179], and state our theorems about whole runs of its compiled AArch64 executable. Each run of this tool performs one operation for one party specified with command-line arguments: keygen creates the party’s keys, init starts a session from a prekey bundle (process_prekey_bundle), and send and recv encrypt and decrypt one message (message_encrypt, message_decrypt). Our theorems cover init, send and recv; keygen is not verified, and the theorems instead take the parties’ keys as part of their setup.

 Rust source: libsignal-cli

 rustlibsignal-cli third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs:L168-L179 ↗ [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179]

 CLI dispatch in fn run for init and send: calls libsignal’s initiate and encrypt and serializes the outgoing wire message.

Call::Init { peer, bundle } => {
 let (me, bundle) = (me?, read_bundle(&bundle, peer.device_id())?);
 let (sessions, identity) = (&mut s.sessions, &mut s.identity);
 accepted(initiate(&peer, &me, sessions, identity, &bundle, now))?;
 Vec::new()
}
Call::Send(peer) => {
 let me = me?;
 let (sessions, identity) = (&mut s.sessions, &mut s.identity);
 let message = accepted(encrypt(input, &peer, &me, sessions, identity, now))?;
 [&[message.message_type() as u8], message.serialize()].concat()
}

 [paper/figures/fig-cli-model.svg]

Figure 3. Each party’s state is a directory, and each operation is one run of the libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] executable in that directory. The environment has access to everything: it runs every call, plays the role of the Signal server by building prekey bundles from the parties’ public/ files, and writes the randomness of every run. The attacker, inside the environment, controls the network and gets a party’s state when it asks to compromise it.

 [paper/figures/fig-cli-state.svg]

Figure 4. Bob’s directory before and after he receives Alice’s first message m1 (abridged; sizes from a real run). The successful recv stores Alice’s identity key and the new session (green) and empties the two one-time prekeys that m1 used (red): files are emptied rather than deleted. Every other file is unchanged. Delivering m1 a second time is rejected and writes no file.

ALICE=8a6f1c2e-3b4d-4e5f-9a0b-1c2d3e4f5a6b.1 BOB=5d3e2f10-7a8b-4c9d-8e0f-a1b2c3d4e5f6.1
run() { head -c 16384 /dev/urandom > $1/coins; (cd $1 && libsignal-cli "${@:2}"); }
bundle() { mkdir $2; cp $1/public/⟨identity key, one prekey of each kind⟩ $2/; }
mkdir alice bob
run bob keygen $BOB 1 # Bob's keys; public halves in bob/public/
run alice keygen $ALICE 0
bundle bob bob_public_bundle # play the server
run alice init $BOB ../bob_public_bundle # PQXDH
printf "Hi Bob!" | run alice send $BOB > m1 # m1: a PreKeySignalMessage
run bob recv $ALICE < m1 # prints Hi Bob!
printf "Hi Alice!" | run bob send $ALICE > r1 # r1: a SignalMessage
run alice recv $BOB < r1 # prints Hi Alice!
run bob recv $ALICE < m1; echo $? # replay: prints 1; no file written

Figure 5. A typical scenario as a bash script, which plays both the environment and the attacker. run writes fresh coins into a party’s directory and runs libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] there; bundle plays Signal’s server, copying a party’s public identity key and one public prekey of each kind into a new directory.

 States. A party’s state is stored in a directory (Figure 3 [#fig-cli-model] and Figure 4 [#fig-cli-state]). Its files hold everything libsignal stores for the party, in libsignal’s own serialization format: its identity key pair, its sessions (one per peer device), its signed, one-time and Kyber1024 prekeys, and the identity keys of its peers. The subdirectory public/ holds what a client uploads to the Signal server: the public halves of its keys and their signatures. Nothing else survives between runs, so leaking a party’s directory is exactly a compromise of its whole state.

 Process semantics. A run depends only on its command line, its standard input, the party’s files, and a file coins that supplies all of its randomness; the clock is fixed and no other entropy source is read. A run produces only an exit code, its standard output, and new contents for the party’s files. Exit code 0 means that libsignal accepted the operation: the changed files are written, and the output (a wire message for send, a plaintext for recv) is on standard output. Exit code 1 means that libsignal rejected it, and then no file is written: libsignal updates its stores one at a time and may fail after some have changed, so, like Signal’s own mobile apps, libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] holds back all store writes until the operation succeeds. (Exit code 2 reports a malformed command line or an unreadable file.) Our theorems speak only about these inputs and outputs, using a Lean model of how Linux loads and runs an AArch64 executable.

 Between runs, control passes to the environment (Figure 3 [#fig-cli-model]). It runs every call of libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179], holds all of the parties’ files, plays Signal’s server by building each prekey bundle as a directory of copies of the peer’s public/ files, and supplies the coins of every run. The attacker lives inside the environment. It decides which party runs which operation, and when. It controls the network: the environment hands it every wire message that a run writes to its standard output, and it chooses what each run reads on its standard input, so it may drop, reorder, replay or modify messages. When it asks to compromise a party, the environment gives it that party’s files. This picture is common to both of our proofs; they differ in how they formalize the environment and the attacker’s capabilities. In the computational proof, the environment is a security game and the attacker is any probabilistic algorithm that makes a bounded number of queries; the security proof bounds its advantage at telling which of two challenge messages was encrypted or getting a forged message accepted. In the symbolic proof, the runs and the environment’s actions are matched by transitions of the DyLean [99 [#bib-wallez2026dylean]] model of the protocol, a compromise leaks the secrets in the party’s files one at a time, and the attacker is a Dolev–Yao attacker that computes on symbolic terms; security is a property of every trace.

 Because libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] is an ordinary executable, the same scenarios can also be run directly from a shell. The script in Figure 5 [#fig-cli-script] plays both the environment and the attacker: it creates Bob and Alice, plays the server to give Alice a bundle of Bob’s keys, runs the PQXDH handshake and two Triple Ratchet messages, and finally, as the attacker, replays Alice’s first message, which Bob rejects without writing any file. We also ran these scenarios on our Lean semantics of the binary (15.6 million instructions across 10 invocations), and every run had the same outcome as on the real binary, including all session state.

 Verification target. We compile libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] and the unmodified libsignal crates with stock rustc (1.98.1) and link them statically with musl using stock LLVM ld.lld into one AArch64 Linux executable (build details in §C.6 [#app-limitations]). The proofs follow each init, send and recv run from the executable’s entry point to its final exit_group system call. The proof must reason instruction by instruction about the CLI and its file-backed stores (including the routines that read files and standard input), musl’s mallocng memory allocator and memcpy/memset, string cloning, the PQXDH, Triple Ratchet and SPQR state machines, key derivation (HKDF and libsignal’s HMAC-SHA256, apart from the hooked SHA-256 compression function), protobuf serialization and MAC verification. The remaining 37 hooked routines are replaced by contracts at the binary boundary, and 18 panic entry points end the run with SIGABRT. The hooked routines are the low-level cryptographic routines (SHA-256 and SHA-512 compression, AES-256-CBC, the X25519 and Edwards curve operations used by XEdDSA, Kyber1024 in PQXDH and ML-KEM-768 and HMAC from libcrux in SPQR); error-message formatting (core::fmt::write and format_inner); three libc memory and string routines (memmove, bcmp and strlen); the two functions that write files and standard output; and the remaining non-crypto Rust library routines not yet de-hooked: UTF-8 checking, memrchr, the fixed clock, an optimization barrier, and the zeroizing destructors of X25519 secrets. In our main theorems, each hooked routine is a fixed Lean function; the cryptographic primitives inside these functions are left abstract. The functional-correctness theorems proved about the binary are shared by the computational and the symbolic security proofs.

 The contracts for the cryptographic routines idealize the primitives, since our results are conditional on their security. The remaining contracts describe the routines’ functional behavior. They are not proved against the real routines, but the scenarios of Figure 5 [#fig-cli-script] have the same outcomes when run with them in the Lean semantics as on the real binary.

 While our setup models a specific execution environment, our results are relevant to many others. For example, the Signal apps for Android and iOS run the same Rust code for libsignal. Nearly all Android phones and all iPhones use AArch64; the difference is in compiler flags, linking, and the operating system interface. The theorems we prove carry over to these other execution environments as long as we trust that they preserve the behavior of the protocol code as exercised by the libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] binary.

 4.1 Binary-Level Guarantees

 The security proofs described in this paper build on a set of theorems about the machine code. These theorems are proved once and for all. They hold for every placement of the code and of musl’s allocator in memory that matches the bytes of the executable, and Lean checks that such placements exist. They also hold for every implementation of the hooked routines.

 These theorems state that the executable implements the Lean models of libsignal on which the security proofs are built. For example, our computational security theorem (through Lemma 4 [#lem-refine]) states that on every init, send and recv run whose inputs are within the stated size limits, the executable does what the model prescribes. If the model accepts the operation, the run exits with code 0, prints the model’s output, and leaves exactly the model’s new state in the party’s files. If the model rejects it, the run exits with code 1, prints nothing, and leaves every file unchanged. If the model panics, the run ends with SIGABRT. The CLI’s own checks are proved as well: a run exits with code 2 if its standard input exceeds 2202^{20}220 bytes, and send rejects plaintexts longer than 10610^6106 bytes. In particular, no run faults, loops forever, or leaves a state that the model would not produce. The symbolic refinement proof is built on the same facts.

 The only exception these theorems allow is a run that exhausts the machine’s memory. A separate theorem shows that this does not happen: in our process model, which gives each run 1 GiB of memory beyond its loaded segments and an 8 MiB stack, no run on inputs within the limits reaches the out-of-memory state. Its proof bounds the heap use of every run through the verified allocator, using the fact that a session record stays below 1.2 MB however many messages the session carries. The security theorems therefore contain no out-of-memory term, and the symbolic theorem states outright that every valid call exits or aborts and never faults or diverges.

 These guarantees concern a fresh process started with only standard input, output and error open, an empty environment, and a fixed clock. The kernel’s random bytes are fixed, so all of a run’s randomness comes from its coins file. The hooked routines are replaced by their contracts. The guarantees say nothing about a machine with less memory, about processes launched with other files open, or about the code of the hooked routines themselves. The security theorems constrain those routines separately.

 5 Verifying Signal

 We verify the security of the libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] binary (§4 [#sec-interface]) in two established models from the research literature: the symbolic model (§5.1 [#sec-sym]) and the computational model (§5.2 [#sec-comp]). In the symbolic model, cryptographic primitives are idealized and output abstract terms. The goal is to show that in every possible execution, secret values (e.g., messages) remain secret and parties agree on whom they are talking to. In the computational model, messages are bit strings and the adversary is a probabilistic algorithm that interacts with the protocol through a bounded number of oracle queries. The proof objective is to show that breaking the protocol is at least as hard as breaking the cryptographic primitives it is built from: any adversary’s advantage in attacking the protocol is bounded by a function of the advantages of efficient reductions that break some underlying cryptographic primitive.

 We work in two independent models for two reasons. First, we wanted to learn whether agents can carry out proofs in both models, which differ in their tools, their methods of reasoning and the ways they fail. Second, the two proofs give different assurances about the protocol. The computational proof bounds the advantage of any query-bounded adversary and accounts for events, such as collisions, that the symbolic proof abstracts away. The symbolic approach, in turn, follows libsignal’s session records more closely and states ten properties under a richer set of compromises. The symbolic proof also covers an attacker (for example, a future quantum computer) that breaks Diffie–Hellman after the handshake. By contrast, our computational proof only covers classical adversaries.

 5.1 Symbolic Proofs

 We prove that the libsignal binary enforces symbolic security properties of the composition of PQXDH and the Triple Ratchet, down to the assembly level. The symbolic model of cryptography simplifies verifying the high-level design of cryptographic protocols by assuming the underlying primitives obey “ideal cryptography” (e.g., no hash collisions, encrypted values are meaningless to attackers without the key). This enables a separation of concerns: developers of the underlying primitives can validate these standard assumptions, while a symbolic analysis of a protocol can rely on them to prove protocol-specific properties.

 Most symbolic security verification efforts verify abstract designs and give no assurance about the real implementation. We instead define and prove symbolic security properties of the compiled binary using the following approach:

 Symbolic Model. We use DyLean [99 [#bib-wallez2026dylean]], an existing Lean framework for symbolic cryptography, to write one composed model of PQXDH and the Triple Ratchet (§5.1.1 [#sec-model]).

 Security Theorems. We state and prove that the model satisfies ten security properties, replicating the symbolic state-of-the-art for Signal [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet], 28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]] (§5.1.2 [#sec-theorems]).

 Assembly-level Symbolic Events. To state symbolic security at the binary level, a thin trusted event layer turns executions of the binary under our AArch64 semantics into hardware traces of protocol events: one event per run of libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179], and one per leak, break or inversion by the attacker between runs (§5.1.3 [#sec-asm-events]).

 Refinement. We prove that every hardware trace is explained by a model trace, up to collisions. A trusted concretization function γ\gammaγ gives the bytes that each model term stands for (§5.1.4 [#sec-refinement]).

 Preservation. We prove that refinement and the model-level theorems together imply the same ten properties, for an attacker that knows only what it observed of the execution, on every hardware trace whose coins never repeat a secret: on the whole trace if it is collision-free, and otherwise on its explained prefix before the collision (i.e., our refinement definition preserves symbolic security; Theorem 1 [#thm-sym], §5.1.5 [#sec-sym-main]).

 The first three items comprise the trusted definitions (§5.3 [#sec-tcb]); everything that connects them (the model-level proofs, refinement and preservation) is proved in Lean.

 [paper/figures/fig-overview.svg]

Figure 6. Overview of the symbolic proofs about libsignal. Refinement shows that every hardware trace of the compiled binary is explained by a model trace, up to collisions, so preservation carries the model’s theorems (Table 2 [#tab-theorems]) over to the binary. Circled numbers match the steps listed above.

 Figure 6 [#fig-overview] shows an overview of this approach. Steps 1 and 2 follow prior work closely. The challenges are in steps 3 and 4, because the binary does not satisfy the symbolic assumptions. First, hardware has no messages: the CPU only reads and writes registers and memory. We address this with a small, trusted augmented program semantics that primarily calls our AArch64 one, but extends it with these events as described in §5.1.3 [#sec-asm-events]. Second, hardware values are finite byte strings, not terms. A Dolev–Yao attacker cannot guess a key, but an attacker of the binary can guess libsignal’s 8-byte MAC with probability 2−642^{-64}2−64, so the binary does not refine the model exactly. Following Arquint et al. [15 [#bib-arquint2023sound]], §5.1.4 [#sec-refinement] maps model terms to bytes but never maps bytes back to terms. A guessed value is then a different term whose bytes happen to collide.

 Crucially, we do not aim to advance the state of the art in what has been proven about the Signal protocol [74 [#bib-marlinspike2013ratcheting], 91 [#bib-libsignal]] at the symbolic level. Our contribution is replicating the state of the art at the symbolic level and rigorously connecting it to the assembly implementation for the first time.

 5.1.1 Symbolic Model

 DyLean [99 [#bib-wallez2026dylean]] is a Lean framework for symbolic protocol analysis in the style of ProVerif [33 [#bib-proverif]] and Tamarin [76 [#bib-meier2013tamarin]]. A protocol is a labeled transition system that produces atomic transitions over a global append-only trace. The transitions are scheduled by the (Dolev–Yao) attacker, who also chooses their inputs. Security theorems are described in terms of the attacker’s knowledge of a trace, which is the least set of terms that contains everything the trace reveals (its network messages, including leaked secrets) and is closed under attacker operations of each primitive. The attacker may inject only terms it knows, so it cannot guess a key or forge a signature.

 Attacker operations and symbolic assumptions are both modeled through equational theories, which model the underlying primitives through trusted (assumed) rewrite rules. For instance, dh [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/EquationalTheory/DiffieHellman.lean#L13] sorts its two secret exponents, so dh [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/EquationalTheory/DiffieHellman.lean#L13](dhPk [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/EquationalTheory/DiffieHellman.lean#L12](a), b) and dh [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/EquationalTheory/DiffieHellman.lean#L13](dhPk [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/EquationalTheory/DiffieHellman.lean#L12](b), a) are the same term, and symDec [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.EquationalTheory.SymEnc/#DY___SymEnc___CanSymEnc___symDec] k iv c returns m exactly when c is symEnc [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.EquationalTheory.SymEnc/#DY___SymEnc___CanSymEnc___symEnc] k iv m and fails otherwise.

 | none => none

 The composed model. Using DyLean, we formalize a single composed symbolic model of the deployed protocol by combining the PQXDH handshake model of Bhargavan et al. [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]] with the Triple Ratchet model of Cheval, Jacomme, and Richards [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet]]. Because the binary must refine it, the model is more faithful to libsignal than the papers’ models in two places: it models X25519’s non-canonical encodings and small subgroup, which are outside the scope of Cheval et al.’s proofs and are the setting of the small-subgroup bug of §6 [#sec-bug-injection]; and it models libsignal’s binding of the KEM public key into the shared secret, rather than the change to the associated data (PQXDH Revision 2) that Bhargavan et al.’s ProVerif proofs assume.

 The composed model is a labeled state machine covering initial prekey bundle processing (with signed and one-time X25519 prekeys and reusable, last-resort Kyber prekeys; one-time Kyber prekeys are not modeled), prekey message reception, and subsequent Triple Ratchet messaging (the Double Ratchet coupled with SPQR), with unboundedly many sessions but at most one active session per party and role, scheduled by a Dolev–Yao attacker that controls the network and may compromise long-term keys, prekeys, or stored session state. Following Bhargavan et al. [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]], the attacker may also break DH (say, with a quantum computer) or KEM (say, with cryptanalysis) and from then on invert public keys of the broken primitive to learn their secrets. §B.1 [#app-sym-model] describes the model as a transition system.

 5.1.2 Security Theorems

 Table 2 [#tab-theorems] lists the theorems we prove. They restate the forward secrecy and post-compromise security properties that Cheval et al. [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet]] prove for the Double Ratchet after PQXDH, and the PQXDH theorems of Bhargavan et al. [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]]; both papers use ProVerif [33 [#bib-proverif]]. All of them hold on every reachable trace of the composed model described above. Neither paper analyzes exactly this protocol, so the last column of the table gives the design-level differences between each paper’s statement and ours. We use libsignal’s names and roles: Alice processes Bob’s prekey bundle and sends the first message, so she is the initiator and Bob the responder. The PQXDH paper swaps the two labels: our initiator secrecy is its responder secrecy (Theorem 7), and our responder secrecy its initiator secrecy (Theorem 9).

 Most of the differences are forced by verifying the deployed code rather than a protocol description: libsignal’s three handshake outputs instead of a single key, KEM public-key binding in the shared secret instead of the associated data, and agreement on prekeys only up to re-encoding. To rule out vacuous theorems, we also prove that honest sessions with and without a one-time prekey are reachable, and that the premises of the forward secrecy and handshake secrecy theorems can be met. At the binary level, we prove that the initial setup and four example interactions (handshakes with and without a one-time prekey, an honest session, and one with a leaked key) arise from runs of any implementation that meets the CLI’s functional specification, and that these runs meet every hypothesis of Theorem 1 [#thm-sym] without a collision.

Table 2. The ten theorems we prove of the composed model (the PQXDH Theorem 1 row has five). Each holds on every reachable trace. The Source column gives the cited paper’s own numbering. Table 5 [#tab-queries] states them precisely.

 Forward secrecy

 Source [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet], Fig. 3, App. A]

 Lean: forward_secrecy [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___ForwardSecrecy___forward_secrecy]

 Statement (informal)

 If the attacker knows the key of a message sent or received on the trace, then that key, a chain key it was derived from, or a root key that derived its chain was compromised.

 Key differences from the source

 Triple Ratchet modeled as in the paper (SPQR key chosen by the attacker and DR shown no less secure). Whereas the paper’s PQXDH composition allows only one session and forbids one-time prekeys, handshake compromises, and ratchet DH-key exposure, we compose with our full PQXDH model: initial root and chain keys are compromised exactly when every handshake deriving them is excused by initiator or responder secrecy, and honest ratchet DH secrets count as compromised when DH is broken. Replay-based FS loss is not ruled out.

 Post-compromise security

 Source [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet], Fig. 4, App. A]

 Lean: post_compromise_security [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___PostCompromiseSecurity___post_compromise_security]

 Statement (informal)

 After a DH ratchet step between two honest DH secrets, a message key derived from it is secret unless a key at or after that step was compromised, or an earlier root key together with one of the two DH secrets.

 Key differences from the source

 As for forward secrecy.

 Initiator secrecy

 Source [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh], Thm. 7]

 Lean: alice_initiator_secrecy [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___Handshake___alice_initiator_secrecy]

 Statement (informal)

 Alice’s handshake key is secret unless (1–2) Bob’s identity key was compromised before the handshake, or at any time together with Bob’s signed prekey and either his Kyber prekey or a KEM break; or (3–4) DH was broken before the handshake, or at any time together with Bob’s Kyber prekey or a KEM break.

 Key differences from the source

 PQXDH composed with the Triple Ratchet instead of an abstract AEAD: the paper’s single key SK\mathit{SK}SK becomes libsignal’s three HKDF outputs (root, chain, and SPQR keys), so secrecy holds for each output separately unless that specific output was exposed from session state.

 Responder secrecy

 Source [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh], Thm. 9]

 Lean: bob_responder_secrecy [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___Handshake___bob_responder_secrecy]

 Statement (informal)

 Bob’s handshake key is secret except in the paper’s seven compromise cases, which also involve Alice’s identity key and Bob’s one-time prekey.

 Key differences from the source

 As for initiator secrecy; also, Bob accepts by verifying the MAC on Alice’s initial Triple Ratchet message (carrying an SPQR header and SPQR-salted message keys) rather than an abstract AEAD tag.

 Authentication

 Source [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh], Thm. 8]

 Lean: authentication [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___Handshake___authentication]

 Statement (informal)

 If Bob accepts a handshake as coming from Alice, then Alice completed an earlier handshake using the same prekeys with some peer (not necessarily Bob), unless Alice’s identity key or Bob’s signed prekey was compromised, or DH broken, before.

 Key differences from the source

 Key confirmation via Alice’s initial Triple Ratchet message, as for responder secrecy. Kyber prekey agreement comes from binding the public key into the KEM shared secret rather than associated data (PQXDH Revision 2). X25519 prekeys agree up to non-canonical re-encoding (yielding identical DH secrets), since the server can re-encode unsigned one-time prekeys and, if Bob’s identity key is compromised, re-sign Bob’s signed prekey.

 PQXDH Theorem 1 corollaries

 Source [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh], Thm. 1, App. A]

 Lean: peer_authentication [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___Handshake___peer_authentication] Lean: handshake_forward_secrecy [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___Handshake___handshake_forward_secrecy] Lean: kci_resistance [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___Handshake___kci_resistance] Lean: session_independence [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___Handshake___session_independence] Lean: hndl_resistance [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.ModelSecurity/#Proven___Handshake___hndl_resistance]

 Statement (informal)

 Peer authentication; handshake forward secrecy (later identity-key compromises reveal nothing); KCI resistance (compromising Bob’s identity key does not let the attacker impersonate Alice to him); session independence (other sessions’ leaked prekeys reveal nothing); harvest-now-decrypt-later resistance (a later DH break, without a KEM break or compromises of the session’s identity keys or prekeys, reveals nothing).

 Key differences from the source

 Stated and proved as five separate theorems rather than informal consequences of Theorems 7–9 (peer authentication and KCI restate Theorem 8). The three secrecy corollaries include the per-key session-state excuse of initiator secrecy.

 5.1.3 Assembly-Layer Events

 To state security at the assembly level, we add protocol events as a thin, trusted layer on top of the unchanged AArch64 semantics. Protocol-level events are naturally observed at the process interface of libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] (§4 [#sec-interface]): its command line, standard streams and files already fix which bytes are a plaintext, a wire message or randomness, so no event needs instrumentation inside the binary. A hardware trace is a list of events. Each records either one run of libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] (init, send or recv) as the environment sees it, or an action of the attacker between runs: leaking a secret from a party’s directory, compromising a party’s DH or KEM, or inverting a public key of an honest party after the matching compromise. The environment may also delete a party’s session file between runs; this emits no event and is how Alice starts a new session. §B.2 [#app-sym-events] lists every event (Table 4 [#tab-events]).

 Lean definition: CliTraces

 inductiveDY.Libsignal.Composed.Refinement.Cli.CliTraces Symbolic.Specifications.Refinement.Statement (L200–222) → [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Refinement.Statement/#DY___Libsignal___Composed___Refinement___Cli___CliTraces]

 Guarded CLI event traces generated by Rel from turn t: session deletions (clear), primitive break/invert events (breakInvert), exited CLI calls preceded by directory leaks (step), and aborted CLI calls terminating the trace (stop).

 5.1.4 Refinement

 Refinement relates hardware traces to traces of the DyLean model. It rests on a concretization function γ\gammaγ, which, following Arquint et al. [15 [#bib-arquint2023sound], §4.2], maps each model term to the byte string it stands for. Because different terms (e.g., a guessed MAC and the real one) can have the same bytes, γ\gammaγ has no inverse, and bytes are never mapped back to terms. γ\gammaγ is a homomorphism: each function symbol of the model comes with a function on byte strings, such as libsignal’s protobuf encoding for wire messages. The cryptographic symbols use an interpretation I\mathcal{I}I, which is universally quantified. The only laws assumed of it are a few trusted facts, chosen so that γ\gammaγ agrees with the equational theory: algebraic and length facts such as Diffie-Hellman commutativity, canonical X25519 public keys, rejection of public keys shifted by a torsion point, and that every AES-CBC ciphertext that decrypts is the encryption of its plaintext. Two further parameters complete γ\gammaγ: byte functions SSS for opaque symbols (SPQR, XEdDSA, Kyber1024 public keys, and decapsulation of non-honest ciphertexts), constrained only to agree with the setup’s provisioned bytes, and a per-trace ghost ggg giving the bytes of each fresh value and of the wire messages’ addresses field, which the model does not represent (§B.3 [#app-sym-gamma]).

 A model trace explains a hardware trace τ\tauτ if it is reachable in the model, its events line up with those of τ\tauτ one to one, in order, with each byte field of a hardware event equal to γ\gammaγ of the corresponding model terms, and it agrees with the execution on details that τ\tauτ does not record, such as how stored session records replace earlier ones (§B.4 [#app-sym-explain]).

 Refinement definition. The standard refinement definition would be trace inclusion: every hardware trace is explained by some model trace. That is false here, because hardware bytes are finite. libsignal truncates its MAC to 8 bytes, so an attacker who guesses the MAC makes the binary accept a forged message. In the model, the receiver accepts only if the received MAC term is the one it computes, and a guessed literal is a different term, so no model trace explains that hardware trace. Our refinement definition is therefore trace inclusion up to collisions, stated in full as follows.

 For every choice WWW of the abstract primitives inside the hooked routines, every interpretation I\mathcal{I}I of the cryptographic symbols that meets their contracts and agrees with WWW, and every well-formed setup rrr (the parties and their provisioned keys):

 ∃S. ∀τ∈TracesI,W(r):(∃ g,ms. ms explains τ under γI,S,g) ∨ CollisionI,S(τ)\exists S.\ \forall \tau \in \mathrm{Traces}_{\mathcal{I},W}(r):\quad \big(\exists\, g, \mathit{ms}.\ \mathit{ms}\ \text{explains}\ \tau\ \text{under}\ \gamma_{\mathcal{I},S,g}\big) \ \vee\ \mathrm{Collision}_{\mathcal{I},S}(\tau)∃S. ∀τ∈TracesI,W​(r):(∃g,ms. ms explains τ under γI,S,g​) ∨ CollisionI,S​(τ)

 Here SSS gives the opaque symbols’ bytes; TracesI,W(r)\mathrm{Traces}_{\mathcal{I},W}(r)TracesI,W​(r) is the set of hardware traces of the binary from setup rrr, with the hooked routines computed from WWW, inversions restricted as in §5.1.3 [#sec-asm-events], and the environment restricted as in §B.7 [#app-sym-scope]; ggg is a ghost and ms\mathit{ms}ms a model trace; and γI,S,g\gamma_{\mathcal{I},S,g}γI,S,g​ is γ\gammaγ with these three parameters fixed. Collision(τ)\mathrm{Collision}(\tau)Collision(τ) says that a prefix of τ\tauτ is explained, and the next run can be explained only through a collision, consisting of two different terms with equal bytes, where at least one term was built by a symbol assumed injective (a fresh value, dhPk [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/EquationalTheory/DiffieHellman.lean#L12], dh [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/EquationalTheory/DiffieHellman.lean#L13], kdfExtract [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/EquationalTheory/KdfExtract.lean#L11], symEnc [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.EquationalTheory.SymEnc/#DY___SymEnc___CanSymEnc___symEnc], trunc8 [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.EquationalTheory.Trunc/#DY___Trunc___CanTrunc8___trunc8], or KEM encapsulation).

 Typical collision cases are a received message that the binary accepts only because its MAC equals the expected one, and a plaintext equal to a secret the attacker does not know. Read the other way around, every hardware trace without a collision is explained by a model trace.

 The collision case is constrained: SSS is chosen before the hardware trace, a received message counts as a collision only if it collides under every explanation of the prefix that has not already read a colliding message, and never if a term the attacker knows explains it without itself colliding. It also excuses a Kyber decapsulation that disagrees with encapsulation, since no Kyber correctness law is assumed on I\mathcal{I}I. This clause depends only on the hooked Kyber routines, Bob’s provisioned Kyber key and the received ciphertext, so it holds only if decapsulation under that key disagrees with encapsulation: the Kyber implementation is incorrect, the provisioned key is malformed (key generation is not verified), or the ciphertext is one of the decryption failures that Kyber1024 has with negligible probability (§B.5 [#app-sym-collision]). Bounding the probability of Collision\mathrm{Collision}Collision is a computational question, outside Lean. Arquint et al. instead assume collision freedom as a hypothesis [15 [#bib-arquint2023sound], §4.3].

 5.1.5 Main Theorem

 Our main symbolic result is that every hardware trace of libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] that does not reach a collision is explained by some model trace, and that all ten theorems of Table 2 [#tab-theorems] hold on every model trace that explains it, against an attacker that knows only what it observed in the hardware trace.

 To state it, let Q(tr,K)Q(\mathit{tr},K)Q(tr,K) be the conjunction of the queries of the theorems of Table 2 [#tab-theorems] on model trace tr\mathit{tr}tr with attacker knowledge KKK. The model-level theorems together state Sec(Model)\mathrm{Sec}(\mathit{Model})Sec(Model): Q(tr,Knows(tr))Q(\mathit{tr}, \mathrm{Knows}(\mathit{tr}))Q(tr,Knows(tr)) for every reachable model trace tr\mathit{tr}tr, where Knows\mathrm{Knows}Knows is the attacker knowledge of §5.1.1 [#sec-model]. The binary-level property Sec(Asm)\mathrm{Sec}(\mathit{Asm})Sec(Asm), where Asm\mathit{Asm}Asm is the machine code of libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179], is the statement of the following theorem.

 Theorem 1 (Binary symbolic security). Lean: signal_session_secure_placed [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.Security/#Proven___Security___signal_session_secure_placed] For every choice WWW of the abstract primitives inside the hooked routines, every interpretation I\mathcal{I}I of the cryptographic symbols that meets their contracts and agrees with WWW, and every well-formed setup rrr (two honest parties, initiator Alice and responder Bob, their provisioned keys, and each party’s pinned copy of the other’s identity key),

 ∃S. ∀τ∈TracesI,W(r) with fresh coins:(Expl(τ)≠∅ ∧ Safe(τ)) ∨ ∃ τ1,e,τ2. τ=τ1 e τ2 ∧ CollisionI,S(τ1,e) ∧ Safe(τ1),\begin{aligned} &\exists S.\ \forall \tau \in \mathrm{Traces}_{\mathcal{I},W}(r) \text{ with fresh coins}:\\ &\quad \big(\mathrm{Expl}(\tau) \neq \emptyset \ \wedge\ \mathrm{Safe}(\tau)\big) \ \vee\ \exists\, \tau_1, e, \tau_2.\ \tau = \tau_1\, e\, \tau_2 \ \wedge\ \mathrm{Collision}_{\mathcal{I},S}(\tau_1, e) \ \wedge\ \mathrm{Safe}(\tau_1), \end{aligned}​∃S. ∀τ∈TracesI,W​(r) with fresh coins:(Expl(τ)=∅ ∧ Safe(τ)) ∨ ∃τ1​,e,τ2​. τ=τ1​eτ2​ ∧ CollisionI,S​(τ1​,e) ∧ Safe(τ1​),​

 where Safe(τ):=∀(g,ms)∈Expl(τ). Q(ms,Kms)\mathrm{Safe}(\tau) := \forall (g, \mathit{ms}) \in \mathrm{Expl}(\tau).\ Q(\mathit{ms}, K_{\mathit{ms}})Safe(τ):=∀(g,ms)∈Expl(τ). Q(ms,Kms​).

 Expl(τ)\mathrm{Expl}(\tau)Expl(τ) is the set of pairs (g,ms)(g, \mathit{ms})(g,ms) such that ms\mathit{ms}ms explains τ\tauτ under γI,S,g\gamma_{\mathcal{I},S,g}γI,S,g​; requiring it to be nonempty stops the ∀\forall∀ from holding vacuously. CollisionI,S(τ1,e)\mathrm{Collision}_{\mathcal{I},S}(\tau_1, e)CollisionI,S​(τ1​,e) says that τ1\tau_1τ1​ is explained and that the next run eee can be explained only through a collision, as in §5.1.4 [#sec-refinement]. A trace has fresh coins if the setup’s X25519 secrets and the secrets that its successful runs draw from their coins (X25519 keys and Kyber encapsulation randomness) are pairwise distinct, compared after X25519 clamping. The theorem also establishes progress: every reachable valid call exits or aborts, and never faults or diverges. The theorem differs from Sec(Model)\mathrm{Sec}(\mathit{Model})Sec(Model) in four ways. First, a trace that reaches a collision gets the guarantee only on its explained prefix before the colliding run. Second, the knowledge KmsK_{\mathit{ms}}Kms​ is the same Dolev–Yao closure, but seeded only with the setup’s public values and the terms matched with what the attacker observed: the bundles, the wire messages, and the leaked or inverted secrets. Third, the hardware traces fix a narrower scenario than the model (§5.1.6 [#sec-sym-limitations]). Fourth, the theorem assumes fresh coins; if the coins come from a CSPRNG, this fails with probability about k2/2251k^2/2^{251}k2/2251 over kkk draws.

 Proof sketch. Lean: comp_refinement_preservation_cli [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Symbolic.Theorems.RefinementPreservation/#Proven___RefinementPreservation___comp_refinement_preservation_cli] The proof is mostly a standard refinement-preservation argument:
