 where Asm⊑Model\mathit{Asm}\sqsubseteq \mathit{Model}Asm⊑Model is the refinement definition of §5.1.4 [#sec-refinement]. Every model trace that explains a hardware trace is reachable, so the model-level theorems hold on it, and the hardware attacker knows no more than the model attacker on that trace. Since each query uses the attacker’s knowledge only as a hypothesis, an attacker that knows less cannot break it. In the collision case, the same argument gives the queries on every explanation of the prefix before the colliding run (§B.8 [#app-sym-pres]).

 5.1.6 Limitations

 Cryptography. Our guarantees are symbolic: they assume perfect cryptography and say nothing about the computational secrecy of bytes. At the binary level, the theorem excuses byte collisions but does not bound their probability, and it trusts the hooked routines of §4 [#sec-interface] to meet their contracts and the low-level cryptographic ones to compute an interpretation that satisfies a few algebraic laws. It also assumes fresh coins (§5.1.5 [#sec-sym-main]). Since no Kyber correctness law is assumed, a run where decapsulation under Bob’s provisioned key disagrees with encapsulation falls under the collision clause and gets no guarantee; this requires an incorrect Kyber implementation, a malformed provisioned key, or a decryption failure that occurs for Kyber1024 with negligible probability (§5.1.4 [#sec-refinement], §B.5 [#app-sym-collision]).

 Scenario. The assembly-level theorem covers a narrower scenario than the model (§B.7 [#app-sym-scope] lists its main restrictions). XEdDSA and SPQR are opaque in γ\gammaγ, so we assume an authentic PKI and SPQR fields copied from honest messages: both parties start with each other’s identity key pinned, so libsignal’s trust-on-first-use path is not exercised, and the bundle Alice receives carries Bob’s genuine identity key, signed prekey and Kyber prekey. The harness provisions two honest parties with fixed roles and one session per session file, and gives Bob one signed prekey, one reusable last-resort Kyber prekey and at most one one-time prekey; one-time Kyber prekeys are not covered. It also bounds input sizes and the number of sends, requires coins files of exactly 192, 64 and 32 bytes for init, send and recv, and restricts Bob after a non-matching prekey message. A run that aborts (for instance on a failed allocation) ends the trace.

 Attacker. Leaks reveal only long-term and prekey secrets and the keys of a party’s current session; side channels and other secrets in memory are out of scope. An exact replay of a prekey message counts as the same session in the model, so our forward secrecy and post-compromise security theorems do not rule out the replay-based loss of forward secrecy described by Cheval et al. [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet], §3.2.2–3.2.3]: the binary runs libsignal’s deduplication of base keys, but the model does not require it.

 Relation to prior analyses. We replicate only the symbolic results of the papers: the computational theorems of Bhargavan et al. have no counterpart here (see §5.2.3 [#sec-comp-thms] for our computational guarantees), and we do not model header encryption, which libsignal does not use. Finally, as in Cheval et al., our ratchet theorems let the attacker choose the SPQR keys, so they show that the Triple Ratchet is no weaker than the Double Ratchet, not the post-quantum healing that SPQR adds. In particular, once DH is broken, at any point in the trace, the ratchet’s DH secrets count as compromised, so post-compromise security claims no healing against an attacker who can break DH, even one who cannot break the KEM.

 5.2 Computational Proofs

 We state computational security as a game [88 [#bib-shoup2004sequences], 22 [#bib-bellare2006codebased]] between a challenger and an adversary. The challenger samples the secrets and holds every party’s state, and the adversary interacts with it through oracles that run protocol steps, corrupt parties and pose challenges. The adversary’s advantage measures how well it does in violating some security property, for example, its probability of getting a forgery accepted, or its probability of guessing a hidden random bit (minus the trivial 1/2 probability of blindly guessing). If the probability of guessing a hidden bit is, say, 1/2+2−641/2 + 2^{-64}1/2+2−64, we conclude that the bit is effectively secret.

 Security is proved via reductions. A reduction RRR to an assumption, such as the hardness of Gap Diffie-Hellman (Gap-DH), is an algorithm that uses the adversary AAA as a subroutine to violate the assumption. RRR runs AAA, answers AAA’s oracle queries itself so that AAA appears to be attacking the protocol, and turns AAA’s advantage into a solution of its own challenge (say, a challenge instance of Gap-DH). A proof shows that the advantage of AAA in attacking the protocol is bounded by some function of the corresponding advantage of various reductions RRR in violating different assumptions, and statistical terms (such as the probability of a collision). If every hardness assumption holds then all reductions have small advantage and we infer that AAA has small advantage in breaking the protocol. Importantly, the reductions must be efficient for the claimed bounds to be meaningful.

 Proofs usually proceed through a sequence of game hops, each a small change to the game justified by one reduction or one statistical argument. What is new in our setting is that the oracles of our games run the compiled libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] executable.

 The proof connects three layers:

 A security game for PQXDH composed with the Triple Ratchet. The adversary runs many handshakes, turns some of them into ratchet sessions, and attacks the confidentiality and authenticity of ratchet messages. It may corrupt long-term keys and ratchet state, and interleave all of these queries arbitrarily.

 A byte-level model of libsignal in Lean. The model follows the Rust source closely, down to encodings, key-derivation labels, key schedules and the SPQR state machine.

 The AArch64 machine code of libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] (§4 [#sec-interface]). Every ratchet step in the game is one run of this executable in the directory of the party concerned, from its entry point to its final exit_group system call.

 We prove that the machine code computes exactly what the model computes, and that the game run on the model is secure. A coupling argument then shows that the game run on the machine code is secure as well (Figure 7 [#fig-comp-overview]).

 [paper/figures/fig-comp-overview.svg]

Figure 7. Overview of our computational proof. Lemma 4 [#lem-refine] establishes that every run of the executable has the outcome the model predicts. A coupling lifts it to the game. Lemma 2 [#lem-session] and Lemma 3 [#lem-comp] bound the model game by the key indistinguishability of its handshake and single-session security. Lemma 1 [#lem-pqxdh] bounds the key indistinguishability of the one-key random-oracle handshake HroH_{\mathrm{ro}}Hro​; one random-oracle idealization step relates it to libsignal’s handshake HlibH_{\mathrm{lib}}Hlib​ (§5.2.3 [#sec-comp-thms]).

 5.2.1 The Security Game

 Our game combines two games from the literature. The handshake oracles are those of the PQXDH key-indistinguishability game of Fiedler and Günther [52 [#bib-fg24]]. They let the adversary register honest parties and parties of its own, generate prekeys, initiate and accept sessions, corrupt identity keys and reveal session keys. The ratchet oracles are those of the secure-messaging game of Alwen, Coretti and Dodis [7 [#bib-alwen2019doubleratchet]] (ACD19). They let the adversary send and deliver messages, pose a challenge (encrypt one of two messages of its choice), inject forged messages and corrupt either party’s ratchet state.

 A single oracle connects the two halves. The adversary may promote a session that it initiated through the game into a ratchet session. The oracle first checks that the handshake used keys that an honest party published, and that no earlier session used the same ephemeral key. It then starts the Triple Ratchet, including SPQR, for both parties with the real session key of the handshake. Finally, it marks the responder’s signed prekey as revealed in the handshake game: the responder’s first ratchet key is that prekey, so the ratchet needs its secret. The ratchet proof therefore does not rely on that secret staying hidden.

 The adversary wins if it guesses which of its two challenge messages was encrypted, or if it gets a forged message accepted. As in ACD19, the game rules out trivial wins, such as decrypting a challenge with state the adversary has just stolen. The game is written once, for any implementation of the handshake and ratchet steps. We write Advcomp(A)\mathsf{Adv}^{\mathrm{comp}}(A)Advcomp(A) for the advantage of adversary AAA in the game run on our Lean model, and Advbin(A)\mathsf{Adv}^{\mathrm{bin}}(A)Advbin(A) for its advantage in the game run on the executable. Our bounds are concrete: they are stated in terms of budgets that limit the numbers of parties, prekeys, sessions and attacker’s queries (Theorem 2 [#thm-main]). §C.1 [#app-game] defines the game precisely.

 5.2.2 Reductions as Programs

 Our theorems assert that efficient reductions exist, so we must make precise what counts as a reduction. One could express reductions as general-purpose Lean functions. However, this choice makes statements of the form “there exists a reduction” vacuous: a Lean function can be noncomputable, for instance by picking the secret key by choice, or arbitrarily expensive.

 We therefore write reductions as programs in a small, deeply embedded reduction language with a cost semantics. A reduction runs the adversary exactly once, as a black box and without rewinding, and answers each of its oracle queries by running a handler. If the assumption game has a challenge phase, the reduction may pause the adversary at one query, obtain the challenge, and then resume the same run.

 A handler computes on ordinary data, samples coins and queries the oracles of the assumption game. It can operate on keys and group elements only through declared primitive operations, and it loops only up to bounds written in the program. The cost of a reduction is computed from its program text alone.

 A reduction is cost-bounded if its cost, its number of oracle queries and the sizes of the values it computes are bounded by explicit polynomials in the game’s budgets, independent of the adversary, and if its program fits a fixed size budget. This rules out a “reduction” that breaks the assumption on its own, for example by exhaustive search or by hard-coding a large table such as a table of discrete logarithms. §C.2 [#app-reductions] gives the cost semantics.

 5.2.3 Main Theorem

 We consider two versions of the PQXDH handshake, both with Kyber1024 as the KEM. The first, HlibH_{\mathrm{lib}}Hlib​, is libsignal’s handshake. Like libsignal, it uses a single Curve25519 scalar per party for both X25519 DH and XEdDSA signatures [79 [#bib-perrin2016xeddsa]], and it derives the session key with HKDF [68 [#bib-rfc5869]], the standard hash-based key-derivation function. The second, HroH_{\mathrm{ro}}Hro​, is the same handshake with that HKDF replaced by a random oracle [21 [#bib-bellare1993random]]: an ideal, truly random function that every party, including the adversary, can query. (Prior analyses [52 [#bib-fg24], 28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]] also model this HKDF as a random oracle.) Our game runs HlibH_{\mathrm{lib}}Hlib​.

 Our main theorem states that the game run on the executable is secure. Concretely, it gives a bound on Advbin(A)\mathsf{Adv}^{\mathrm{bin}}(A)Advbin(A), the advantage of any adversary AAA that respects the budgets. The bound is a sum of two kinds of terms. The first kind is the advantage of a reduction, built from AAA, against one of our cryptographic assumptions. The second kind is a statistical term, such as the probability of a collision or of a decryption failure. One term, εmlkem(A)\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mlkem}}(A)εmlkem​(A), depends on the adversary itself (see below). The reductions are fixed by the theorem and are cost-bounded, so if every assumption holds, every term is small and no efficient adversary breaks the executable. Clause 1 states this bound for libsignal’s handshake as is. Clause 2 tightens the handshake term when the handshake’s HKDF is modeled as a random oracle.

 Theorem 2 (Binary security, simplified). Lean: libsignal_cli_pq_secure_elf_binary_placed [Tier-1___-statements/Theorem-files/Computational.Theorems.Main/#CryptoVerification___LibsignalCli___libsignal_cli_pq_secure_elf_binary_placed] Let NidN_{\mathrm{id}}Nid​ be the number of honest parties, NspkN_{\mathrm{spk}}Nspk​ and NopkN_{\mathrm{opk}}Nopk​ the numbers of signed and one-time prekeys, NsendN_{\mathrm{send}}Nsend​ the number of initiated sessions (of which Nsend,opkN_{\mathrm{send,opk}}Nsend,opk​ use a one-time prekey), qonq_{\mathrm{on}}qon​ the number of handshake queries other than random-oracle queries, qstartq_{\mathrm{start}}qstart​ the number of promoted sessions, and qkq_kqk​ the number of ratchet queries in the kkk-th session.

 If HKDF is modeled as a random oracle in the handshake, then the bound holds with εKI\varepsilon_{\mathsf{KI}}εKI​ replaced by βKIδ+εro\beta_{\mathsf{KI}}^{\delta}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ro}}βKIδ​+εro​ (defined below).

 theorem libsignal_cli_pq_secure_elf_binary_placed :
 CliPqxdhBinaryCorrectAndSecurePlaced [Tier-1___-statements/Statement-files-_LPAR_T_RPAR_/Computational.Specifications.LibsignalCli.StatementPqElf/#CryptoVerification___LibsignalCli___CliPqxdhBinaryCorrectAndSecurePlaced] cliElfBytes [Tier-1___-statements/Statement-files-_LPAR_T_RPAR_/LibsignalBinary.Specifications.SignalSession.CliElf/#CryptoVerification___SignalSession___cliElfBytes] := ⋯

 The terms of the bound are as follows.

 εKI\varepsilon_{\mathsf{KI}}εKI​ is the key-indistinguishability advantage, against HlibH_{\mathrm{lib}}Hlib​, of a reduction run with AAA. Under the random-oracle idealization of clause 2, εro\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ro}}εro​ is the gap between HlibH_{\mathrm{lib}}Hlib​ and HroH_{\mathrm{ro}}Hro​, and βKIδ=βKI+2 NsendNspk δkem\beta_{\mathsf{KI}}^{\delta}=\beta_{\mathsf{KI}}+2\,N_{\mathrm{send}}N_{\mathrm{spk}}\,\delta_{\mathrm{kem}}βKIδ​=βKI​+2Nsend​Nspk​δkem​ bounds the key indistinguishability of HroH_{\mathrm{ro}}Hro​. Here βKI\beta_{\mathsf{KI}}βKI​ is a sum of the advantages εSig\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Sig}}εSig​, εShCr\varepsilon_{\mathrm{ShCr}}εShCr​ and εGapDH\varepsilon_{\mathrm{GapDH}}εGapDH​ of cost-bounded reductions against unforgeability of XEdDSA, collision resistance of Kyber1024 (SH-CR [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]]) and Gap-DH on X25519, plus public-key collision terms. Each advantage is multiplied by the number of parties, prekeys or sessions that the adversary’s online handshake queries can touch, or by products of these numbers, so the term grows with the adversary’s activity rather than with the size of the deployment (Lemma 1 [#lem-pqxdh] gives βKI\beta_{\mathsf{KI}}βKI​ in full).

 Sessk\mathsf{Sess}_kSessk​ bounds the advantage of the adversary in the kkk-th ratchet session as if that key were uniform; the term 2εKI2\varepsilon_{\mathsf{KI}}2εKI​ pays for replacing the real handshake keys by uniform ones. Following ACD19 [7 [#bib-alwen2019doubleratchet]], it sums the advantages of cost-bounded reductions against the continuous key agreement (CKA) of the DH ratchet, the root key derivation as a PRF-PRNG, and the forward-secure encryption of messages, with further terms for SPQR’s use of HKDF. The remaining terms are statistical. The largest is the probability of forging libsignal’s 8-byte MAC tag, at least 2−642^{-64}2−64 per attempt, which grows quadratically in both the number of messages and the number of epochs of the session. §C.9 [#app-sess] gives Sessk\mathsf{Sess}_kSessk​ in full.

 For δkem\delta_{\mathrm{kem}}δkem​, the Kyber specification gives the heuristic estimate δkem≤2−174\delta_{\mathrm{kem}}\le 2^{-174}δkem​≤2−174.

 εmlkem(A)\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mlkem}}(A)εmlkem​(A) is the probability that, in the game run with AAA, the coins of one or two sends, honest or chosen by AAA, give an ML-KEM-768 key pair and encapsulation whose decapsulation fails. Since AAA chooses the coins of the sends it injects, this term depends on AAA: it is small unless AAA can find ML-KEM-768 decryption failures. It is not the advantage of a reduction, because a reduction fixed in advance could hard-code a failing pair.

 Proof sketch. The proof has four main parts, stated as lemmas in §C.3 [#app-lemmas].

 PQXDH (Lemma 1 [#lem-pqxdh]) bounds the key indistinguishability of HroH_{\mathrm{ro}}Hro​ by βKIδ\beta_{\mathsf{KI}}^{\delta}βKIδ​. Its proof is a sequence of game hops in the style of [52 [#bib-fg24]].

 Single session (Lemma 2 [#lem-session]) bounds the advantage of an adversary against one ratchet session, started from a uniform key, by Sess\mathsf{Sess}Sess.

 Composition (Lemma 3 [#lem-comp]) reduces the game run on the model to the key indistinguishability of the handshake and to one single-session game per promoted session, by a hybrid argument over the sessions.

 Refinement (Lemma 4 [#lem-refine]) states that every run of the executable has the model’s outcome, unless the machine runs out of memory. Its proof is compositional, in a separation logic for AArch64. Each compiled function, from musl’s start-up code and its mallocng allocator through the CLI, protobuf, libsignal and SPQR, gets a contract that relates its registers and memory to the corresponding model function. The contracts are proved by symbolic execution. The lemma holds for every placement of the code in memory that matches the executable, so addresses and relocations are checked against its bytes rather than trusted from the linker.

 The theorem follows from the four lemmas by coupling the two games. The game on the executable stores directories where the game on the model stores model states, and the coupling maintains that each directory is the encoding of the corresponding state. By Lemma 4 [#lem-refine], together with a proof that no run in the game exhausts memory (§4.1 [#sec-binary-guarantees]), this invariant breaks only if a Kyber1024 decapsulation fails in an honest handshake, which gives the δkem\delta_{\mathrm{kem}}δkem​ term. Otherwise, the adversary wins both games with the same probability. ML-KEM-768 decryption failures in SPQR are charged once for the whole game: the Lean proof carries out the composition in a game that stops at the first such failure and bounds the probability of reaching it by 2 εmlkem(A)2\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mlkem}}(A)2εmlkem​(A).

 5.2.4 Assumptions and Idealizations

 Cryptographic assumptions. All but two of our assumptions are standard. The two exceptions arise because libsignal uses each party’s long-term Curve25519 secret key for two purposes: to compute X25519 Diffie-Hellman shared secrets in the handshake, and to sign the party’s prekeys with XEdDSA. Standard assumptions treat each primitive on its own, with its own key, and say nothing about whether using one key for both could help an attacker. For example, signatures on chosen messages might help it compute DH secrets, or DH results might help it forge signatures. We therefore use joint-security assumptions [46 [#bib-degabriele2012joint], 95 [#bib-thormarker2021ed25519]]: XEdDSA signatures remain unforgeable even for an attacker that can also obtain DH results for the same key, and Gap-DH remains hard even for an attacker that can also obtain signatures under it.

 Outside the trusted base, we prove in Lean that both joint assumptions reduce to standard Gap-DH when SHA-512 is modeled as a random oracle (§C.10 [#app-xeddsa-rom]). The main theorem keeps the joint assumptions, because deriving them from Gap-DH would weaken the bound considerably. Finally, ML-KEM-768 decryption failures are not covered by an assumption; the bound charges them directly as εmlkem(A)\varepsilon_{\mathrm{mlkem}}(A)εmlkem​(A) (§5.2.3 [#sec-comp-thms]).

 Random oracle. Clause 2 of the theorem treats the handshake’s HKDF as a random oracle. This is a heuristic: real HKDF is a fixed function, so clause 2 is not a theorem about it. Clause 1 makes no such idealization, and none of the ratchet’s key derivations are idealized in either clause.

 Environment. The routines that libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] calls through contracts (§4 [#sec-interface]) are assumed to behave as their Lean specifications say. The theorem also assumes functional properties of the primitives, such as that decryption inverts encryption, and fixes features of the process environment, such as the clock, the kernel’s random bytes, the stack size and the available memory (§4.1 [#sec-binary-guarantees]). §C.5 [#app-assm] lists these assumptions. Running out of memory is not assumed away: a proof shows that no run in the game exhausts the available memory (§4.1 [#sec-binary-guarantees]).

 5.2.5 Limitations

 Our bounds give no guarantee against a quantum adversary. The symbolic proof covers part of this gap (§5.1 [#sec-sym]). The game also restricts the adversary in ways that the literature does not. The most significant restriction is that the adversary cannot inject its own PQXDH initial message to the responder of a session that is later promoted. Further, the theorem does not cover the privacy of the payload, or the authentication of the responder, for first messages of handshakes that are never promoted. As in ACD19, corruption is refused at some points, for instance while an uncompromised message is in transit. Our game also refuses it until the initiator reaches epoch 10, since until then she may still hold the receiving chain for the leaked signed prekey.

 The setting is simplified as well. Each promotion starts one session, messages have a fixed length, and there is no multi-device state. Identity keys are trusted on first use. The statistical terms limit the bound to moderate scale: if nearly every message starts a new epoch, they make it vacuous for single sessions of about 25,00025{,}00025,000 messages, or for 2202^{20}220 sessions of 2102^{10}210 messages.

 We note that tightening bounds like this is a natural task for AI. Because the theorem’s statement is a Lean definition, a tighter bound is a change to the specification, which humans review, followed by a new proof campaign, which agents carry out.

 Finally, the results concern our build of libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179], run as a fresh process on a machine with the memory of §4.1 [#sec-binary-guarantees], not Signal’s own applications, and some of the environment assumptions are argued informally. §C.6 [#app-limitations] lists the remaining restrictions.

 5.3 Trusted Computing Base

 The trusted computing base (TCB) is the code that must be audited in order to have confidence that the theorems provide the security guarantees that we claim. To believe our theorems, a reader must check their statements and, transitively, every definition they use. For the symbolic proof, this is the DyLean model and its security statements, the event layer, the concretization function γ\gammaγ, the refinement statement with its collision clause, and the environment’s restrictions (§5.1.6 [#sec-sym-limitations]). The computational proof trusts the definitions of the game, the assumption games and the bound, the Lean model of libsignal and SPQR, and the random-oracle step (§5.2.4 [#sec-assm]).

 Both proofs trust (a) the AArch64 semantics and the Lean model of how Linux runs a process (§2.2 [#sec-aarch64], §4.1 [#sec-binary-guarantees]), (b) the contracts of the hooked routines (§4 [#sec-interface]), and (c) the functional and algebraic properties assumed of the cryptographic primitives. These are definitions that are transitively included in the theorem statements. The TCB also includes the tooling that extracts symbol names from the compiler’s object files, and the scripts that build the binary in a reproducible way (§C.6 [#app-limitations]).

 Everything else is checked by Lean, including the model-level proofs, the refinement, preservation and coupling arguments, the game hops and reductions, the function contracts and the program logic. For this reason, trusting our proofs also requires trusting Lean itself. The Lean community has invested significantly in trustworthiness of the Lean system, including the design of a small, carefully audited kernel, as well as independent kernels and consistency proofs [36 [#bib-carneiro2019lean], 37 [#bib-carneiro2024lean4lean], 16 [#bib-nanoda]]. Notably, however, the Rust compiler is not trusted. An error in any of the untrusted components that compromises the security properties that we formalize, including a bug in the compiler, or a bug in the Rust code, would cause the theorems to become false, so that the proof would fail.

 6 Finding Historical Bugs

 To check whether our specifications are strong enough to catch real bugs, we reintroduced two bugs that Cheval et al. [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet]] found in libsignal and that are now fixed. We injected each bug by reverse-applying its upstream fix commit to the libsignal v0.105.4 source and recompiling the binary, which we call Asmbug\mathit{Asm}_{\mathrm{bug}}Asmbug​.

 Weak state [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet], §3.2.1], fix cd36118 [https://github.com/signalapp/libsignal/commit/cd361186fba60c83186262d91b03af608fb8c7c3]: a prekey message that fails to decrypt on its new session is trial-decrypted with older sessions, and if that succeeds the new session’s unconfirmed state, which holds a copy of the signed prekey secret, is stored.

 Small subgroup [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet], §3.2.2], fix 5293caa [https://github.com/signalapp/libsignal/commit/5293caa6cad81566b80f3c3731857bedaa78737b]: the PQXDH responder does not check that the initiator’s X25519 base key is torsion-free and in range, so a replayed handshake with a mangled base key creates a second session with the same shared secret.

 For both bugs we have complete Lean proofs that the symbolic security theorem (§5.1.4 [#sec-refinement]) does not hold of the bug-injected binary, ¬Sec(Asmbug)\neg\mathrm{Sec}(\mathit{Asm}_{\mathrm{bug}})¬Sec(Asmbug​). Recall that Sec(Asm)\mathrm{Sec}(\mathit{Asm})Sec(Asm) requires that, for every extern interpretation I\mathcal{I}I meeting the contracts and every well-formed setup rrr, there exist opaque-symbol byte functions SSS such that every collision-free hardware trace τ∈TracesI,W(r)\tau \in \mathrm{Traces}_{\mathcal{I},W}(r)τ∈TracesI,W​(r) is explained by at least one reachable model trace (Expl(τ)≠∅\mathrm{Expl}(\tau) \neq \emptysetExpl(τ)=∅) and satisfies all ten security queries of Table 2 [#tab-theorems] on every such explanation. Each disproof exhibits concrete witness inputs and splits into two parts: a specification part showing that, under a valid extern interpretation I\mathcal{I}I and well-formed setup rrr, no choice of SSS either explains the resulting hardware trace τ\tauτ, which has fresh coins, in the model or excuses it as a collision; and an implementation part showing that Asmbug\mathit{Asm}_{\mathrm{bug}}Asmbug​ produces τ\tauτ under the AArch64 semantics (§2.2 [#sec-aarch64]).

 For the small-subgroup bug, Alice initiates a session and sends a prekey message whose X25519 base key the attacker modifies by setting bit 255, giving a distinct encoding with the same Diffie-Hellman outputs; the model’s canonical-key check rejects this message, whereas Asmbug\mathit{Asm}_{\mathrm{bug}}Asmbug​ accepts it and stores a session. For the weak-state bug, Alice establishes a session with Bob (leaving skipped message keys in Bob’s session file) and then initiates a second session; the attacker splices the second session’s PQXDH header onto an inner message from the first session, which Asmbug\mathit{Asm}_{\mathrm{bug}}Asmbug​ accepts via trial decryption against the stored session while no model transition explains the run. Both disproofs need no assumptions beyond Lean’s standard axioms: the Lean kernel parses the SHA-256-pinned ELF binary Asmbug\mathit{Asm}_{\mathrm{bug}}Asmbug​ and steps its AArch64 machine code across the runs of each witness trace.

 On the other hand, refuting the computational theorem is much harder. The theorem states that there exist cost-bounded reductions, against hardness assumptions such as Gap Diffie-Hellman, whose success probabilities bound the advantage of every adversary. To refute it, one must exhibit an adversary that exploits the bug for which every cost-bounded reduction rarely succeeds, which amounts to proving the hardness assumptions themselves, an open problem.

 7 AlphaProof Code in Practice

 We now describe how we used AlphaProof Code in our verification of libsignal. The proof work was done by a team of five over four weeks, working in parallel on symbolic and computational proofs. Each of us ran agents nonstop on our own workstation, directing specification work and launching proof campaigns on different parts of the system.

 Scale. The repository contains nearly 7 million lines of Lean, counting comments and blank lines. About 6.5 million of them are proofs: 5.3 million about the binary, which both security proofs share, about 860,000 for the computational proof and about 350,000 for the symbolic proof. Another 140,000 lines prove the reintroduced bugs (§6 [#sec-bug-injection]), and 70,000 verify the allocator. The verified libsignal code is about 560 KB, about 140,000 AArch64 instructions. The specification directories hold about 54,000 lines, but only part of them is trusted: every file is marked as trusted, trusted in part, or checked by Lean. Reading every definition that our headline theorems depend on means reading about 18,000 lines of code in the repository (about 28,000 with documentation), plus about 8,000 lines of the AArch64 semantics and a Lean representation of the compiled machine code (about 95,000 lines), which a tool extracts from the compiler’s object files; Lean checks its code against the executable’s bytes, so only the symbol names it assigns need to be trusted.

 Writing specifications. The most important lesson from our experience is that, once proofs become cheap, the hard question is what to prove. The majority of our effort went not into proofs but into specifications. This trend is perhaps apparent in software development more broadly, but it is particularly acute for cryptographic protocols, where an entire literature is devoted to the question of how to formalize security (§8 [#sec-related]). One possible future is that programmers write formal security specifications, natural-language intent and performance constraints, and AI generates, optimizes and proves the implementation.

 Our specifications were developed through an intense back-and-forth between humans and agents powered by Gemini 4 Carbon and Claude Opus 5.5. For each component—for example, the binary contracts of a libsignal entry point, or the computational main theorem—the specification writer drafted a small set of top-level statements, following a model from the literature where one existed. An agent from a different model family then reviewed the draft for faithfulness to the source paper and to libsignal, for strength (would it rule out known or plausible bugs?), and, where it goes beyond the literature, for consistency with the literature’s conventions. We gave many rounds of targeted feedback before approving a specification and pinning it as the target for proofs. Left to themselves, agents produced specifications that were hard to audit; we sometimes directed rewrites for legibility, which paid off when new team members joined late to review them.

 Writing specifications against real code also turned out to be a stress test for the literature. For both formalizations we record every place where the Lean statements depart from their reference papers or from libsignal, together with its effect on the result; the computational catalog has over 200 entries and the symbolic one about 35. Most are routine: the same idea stated differently in Lean, a place where libsignal differs from the scheme analyzed on paper, or a restriction the papers needed for their tools to terminate, which our symbolic model lifts. Some, however, are errors in the published analyses that we had to repair before the proofs could go through. Fifteen are gaps or bugs in the computational analysis of the Double Ratchet [7 [#bib-alwen2019doubleratchet]], such as missing hypotheses and security game conditions that allow trivial wins. On the symbolic side, the printed ProVerif queries of Cheval, Jacomme and Richards [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet]] look up skipped message keys at the wrong ratchet index, which makes receiver-side forward secrecy false as printed, leave a variable unbound, and bracket post-compromise security so that exposing a party’s own DH key, together with any root-key compromise, excuses a message regardless of when the heal happened. In the PQXDH analysis of Bhargavan et al. [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]], reading “some SPKB\mathsf{SPK}_BSPKB​ was compromised” literally makes the session-independence corollary unprovable; we bind it to the session’s own signed prekey, as their ProVerif query does.

 Generating proofs. The proofs were produced entirely by agent teams powered by Gemini 4 Carbon (§2.3 [#sec-proof]), and none of the authors wrote any proof code. Keeping dozens of agents productive for days at a time is an engineering problem in its own right, and much of our early effort went into the skills that define the agent team. We developed them iteratively by watching campaigns run. A separate agent, above the team orchestrator, serves only as the interface between us and the team: it monitors the team, and we used its logs together with it to diagnose bottlenecks and propose fixes to its processes. Early on, monitoring the team took a lot of human time; this dropped as the skills matured. In the near future, we expect teams like this one to improve themselves.

 We summarize a few improvements that emerged through human-AI iterative co-evolution in this way. Agents share notices through a common bulletin file rather than repeating messages. Team leads try to refute their goals before assigning work, fix shared parameters such as stack bounds across callees, and point workers to existing lemmas. Support agents batch expensive Lean builds to fit in memory, watch for stalled agents and exhausted model quotas, and refactor slow modules. The orchestrator reshapes the team as the campaign evolves. Every new invariant an agent introduces must come with a witness that it holds on some reachable state, so that agents cannot make progress by proving vacuous lemmas.

 While no human wrote proof code, compute latency was an issue. A clean build of our proofs could take several hours, and early in the project, small source changes or a recompiled binary required large proof repairs. Better program-semantics infrastructure later reduced this. We see substantial room for optimization of proofs generated by and proof techniques deployed by AI.

 Preventing specification weakening. Agents under pressure to finish a goal will sometimes weaken it [107 [#bib-zhong2025impossiblebench], 78 [#bib-paglieri2026swarm]], for example by adding a hypothesis or narrowing a precondition, and this can happen indirectly through a definition that the statement depends on: in one swarm of Lean-proving agents, an agent redefined a predicate used in a conjecture’s hypothesis as False, and the trick spread to the rest of the swarm [78 [#bib-paglieri2026swarm]]. We therefore never rely on an agent’s own report that a goal is done. The gatekeeper (§2.4 [#sec-gatekeeper]) sees only file paths and theorem names, and none of the provers’ dialogue or explanations; Lean replays every proof in a fresh environment, and sorry and native_decide are rejected.

 At the same time, freezing the first draft of a specification is too rigid. Even experienced specification writers rarely get every detail right on the first try, and first drafts are often overly conservative in benign ways that make them false of the real code. Our solution is the amendment process of §2.3 [#sec-proof]. An amendment is allowed only if the proof cannot instead be completed with a stronger invariant or a different decomposition, and only if it keeps the non-vacuity witnesses and honest runs covered. A neutral amendment must bring the specification closer to the code. A proposal that would weaken security to match the code is never accepted as an amendment; it is raised to a human as a potential bug. Accepted amendments are small and logged with their justification on an audited branch. Amendments that change trusted assumptions are approved during the campaign, by one of us or under a policy we set (§2.3 [#sec-proof]), and all amendments are reviewed by us at the end of each campaign. One example is extending the composed symbolic query’s handshake-compromise exception from the initial root key to the initial chain key derived alongside it.

 The human role. To summarize, we decided the scope and order of the work, chose the reference models, and guided and approved the trusted statements. We also designed the structure of the specifications and guided the overall proof strategy: for example, writing reductions in a small reduction language with a cost semantics (§5.2.2 [#sec-reductions]), and, for the symbolic proof, an event layer and concretization homomorphism that maps binary executions to protocol events, together with a refinement to a DyLean model up to collisions that preserves the security properties (§5.1.4 [#sec-refinement]). During proof campaigns, we reviewed escalated specification issues and potential bugs, adjudicated amendments that change trusted assumptions, and monitored progress through status logs. We also designed the skills that define the agent team, and refined them as we observed the agents’ behavior. We did not write proofs. Every statement we present as a theorem is checked by Lean, and every assumption we rely on is listed among the trusted definitions (§5.3 [#sec-tcb]).

 8 Related Work

 Analyses of the Signal protocol design. Kobeissi, Bhargavan and Blanchet [67 [#bib-signalstar]] analyzed a variant of Signal with ProVerif and CryptoVerif. Bhargavan et al. [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]] verified PQXDH with ProVerif and CryptoVerif, finding issues in an early version of its specification. Cheval, Jacomme and Richards [40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet]] gave an automated analysis of the Double Ratchet composed with PQXDH, found the two libsignal bugs we re-inject in §6 [#sec-bug-injection], and proved forward secrecy and post-compromise security. Our symbolic theorems restate theirs and those of Bhargavan et al. with adaptation to connect to our libsignal-cli [https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification/blob/808b4b7ffef198e6a91f71ed776d641a1c236882/third_party/libsignal/libsignal-session/cli/src/lib.rs#L168-L179] binary.

 On the computational side, Cohn-Gordon et al. [42 [#bib-cgcdgs17], 43 [#bib-cohngordon2020signal]] gave the first game-based analysis of Signal’s X3DH handshake and Double Ratchet as a multi-stage key exchange. Alwen, Coretti and Dodis [7 [#bib-alwen2019doubleratchet]] defined secure messaging with immediate decryption and proved the Double Ratchet secure in a modular way. Our ratchet game follows their definitions.

 Fiedler and Günther [52 [#bib-fg24]] analyzed PQXDH in a key-indistinguishability game, which our handshake oracles follow. Both Fiedler and Günther [52 [#bib-fg24]] and Bhargavan et al. [28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh]] give each party separate Diffie-Hellman and signing identity keys; Lemma 1 [#lem-pqxdh] bounds the one-key handshake of libsignal under joint X25519 Gap-DH and XEdDSA unforgeability assumptions [46 [#bib-degabriele2012joint], 95 [#bib-thormarker2021ed25519], 35 [#bib-brendel2021ed25519]], while standalone Lean proofs for the two-key key-indistinguishability and first-message authentication models remain in the artifact outside the trusted base.

 Verified protocol implementations. Several projects verify implementations rather than designs. miTLS and Project Everest [27 [#bib-bhargavan2013mitls]] implemented TLS in F#\## and proved computational security of the implementation, Noise⋆^\star⋆ [59 [#bib-ho2022noise]] generates verified implementations of Noise protocols, and LibSignal⋆^\star⋆ [83 [#bib-protzenko2019wasm]] is a verified implementation of the Signal protocol written in F⋆^\star⋆ and compiled to WebAssembly. These are new implementations written for verification; LibSignal⋆^\star⋆, in particular, is separate from the production libsignal.

 Deployed code has been verified for narrower properties: the s2n TLS library is continuously verified with SAW [41 [#bib-chudnov2018continuous]] for functional correctness of its HMAC, DRBG and handshake state machine, and SPQR’s production Rust crate is proved panic-free with hax [25 [#bib-bhargavan2024hax], 90 [#bib-cryspen2025spqr]], alongside ProVerif models of its design [89 [#bib-spqr]]. Neither proves protocol-level security of the code. One line of work extracts a model from implementation code and verifies the model: fs2pv [26 [#bib-bhargavan2008fs2pv]] translates F# code into ProVerif models, Aizatulin et al. [2 [#bib-aizatulin2011csec]] extract ProVerif models from C code by symbolic execution, ASPIER [38 [#bib-chaki2009aspier]] abstracts C code into a model that it checks for a bounded number of sessions, and Kobeissi et al. [67 [#bib-signalstar]] verified a JavaScript implementation of a Signal variant by translating it to ProVerif and CryptoVerif models.

 Notably, another line of work annotates the source code itself with specifications and proves them with a type checker or program verifier. Arquint et al. [15 [#bib-arquint2023sound]] verify annotated Go and Java implementations against a Tamarin model, with a soundness argument that maps model terms to bytes; we follow their concretization in our symbolic refinement. Their main case study gives an end-to-end symbolically verified implementation of the WireGuard key exchange, derived from the official Go implementation of this widely deployed VPN and interoperable with it. To make verification tractable, they modified that code: they removed DDoS protection, metrics and load balancing, and adapted it around stubs for cryptographic and network operations. We verify the libsignal code unmodified. Dupressoir et al. [50 [#bib-dupressoir2011guiding]] prove protocol properties of annotated C code with a general-purpose verifier.

 All of these work either on the source level, on code written or modified for verification, on code in a verification-friendly language, or with a model extracted from source, so the guarantee for the running program relies on a trusted compiler, extraction step or language semantics. We verify the unmodified production library as it is compiled by the stock Rust compiler in a CLI harness (with hooked cryptographic primitives), and state our theorems over machine code. This removes the compiler and the Rust semantics from the trusted base, at the price of reasoning about optimized AArch64.

 Mechanized symbolic model cryptography. Symbolic analyses make idealized assumptions about the underlying cryptographic primitives and model the attacker as a Dolev–Yao network adversary that manipulates messages as terms [49 [#bib-dolev1983security]]. This focuses the analysis on the protocol design rather than the cryptographic algorithms employed by the protocol. In ProVerif [30 [#bib-blanchet2001proverif], 32 [#bib-blanchet2016proverif]], protocols are specified in an applied pi-calculus (process calculus), and proofs are done by translation into Horn clauses, and attacker knowledge is represented as a predicate over these clauses. It permits reasoning about an unbounded number of sessions, but false positives are possible. Tamarin [76 [#bib-meier2013tamarin], 86 [#bib-schmidt2012dh]] models state as multisets, protocols as multiset rewrite rules, and attacker knowledge as one kind of fact over multisets. Properties are stated in first-order logic over traces. Proofs are solved by constraint solving either automatically or auto-actively. DY⋆^\star⋆ [24 [#bib-bhargavan2021dystar]] proves trace invariants of executable protocol code in F⋆^\star⋆, and two recent Lean frameworks build on its approach: DyLean [99 [#bib-wallez2026dylean]], which adds user-defined equational theories and threat models, and LeanDY [63 [#bib-jeanteur2026leandy]], which combines type-based and trace-based reasoning. Our symbolic specifications and proofs use DyLean, so the protocol model, its security proofs and the AArch64 semantics live in one logic, and Lean’s kernel checks the refinement between them.

 Mechanized computational cryptography. Game-based proofs [88 [#bib-shoup2004sequences], 22 [#bib-bellare2006codebased]] have been mechanized in CertiCrypt [18 [#bib-barthe2009certicrypt]], EasyCrypt [17 [#bib-barthe2011easycrypt]], FCF [81 [#bib-petcher2015fcf]], CryptHOL [19 [#bib-basin2020crypthol]] and SSProve [1 [#bib-abate2021ssprove]], among others, and CryptoVerif [31 [#bib-blanchet2008cryptoverif]] automates such proofs. Our development builds on VCVio [97 [#bib-vcvio]], a library for oracle computations in Lean. These frameworks handle the efficiency of reductions in different ways. Some leave it informal, and others restrict reductions syntactically. We write reductions in a small costed language and prove explicit cost bounds (§5.2.2 [#sec-reductions]).

 Verified cryptographic code. Verified implementations of cryptographic primitives (as opposed to protocols) are complementary to our work, since they specify and verify functional correctness properties (and sometimes constant-time execution) of the primitives that we use in our verification of a cryptographic protocol. Many prior works have verified these primitives, at the C or assembly level, including HACL⋆^\star⋆ [108 [#bib-zinzindohoue2017hacl]], Fiat Cryptography [51 [#bib-erbsen2019fiat]], Vale [34 [#bib-bond2017vale]], Jasmin [4 [#bib-almeida2017jasmin]], the formally verified ML-KEM in libcrux [44 [#bib-libcrux]], and the s2n-bignum and AWS-LC proofs in HOL Light [8 [#bib-s2nbignum]]. Computational security proofs of cryptographic primitives have been carried down to assembly through verified compilers [5 [#bib-almeida2013certified], 6 [#bib-almeida2020lastmile], 3 [#bib-almeida2024kyber]]. To our knowledge, ours are the first proofs of protocol-level security properties for the unmodified code of a widely deployed implementation of a cryptographic protocol.

 Verifying code at the binary level. Our AArch64 semantics belongs to a line of work on formal ISA models, including Sail [14 [#bib-armstrong2019sail]], which we test against, Islaris [85 [#bib-sammler2022islaris]], which verifies machine code against such models, and LNSym [55 [#bib-lnsym]], an Armv8 symbolic simulator in Lean 4. CompCert [73 [#bib-leroy2009compcert]] and the translation validation of seL4 [87 [#bib-sewell2013translation]] address the gap between source and machine code by verifying the compiler or each compilation. Neither is available for Rust, and source-level Rust verifiers such as hax [25 [#bib-bhargavan2024hax]], Aeneas [58 [#bib-ho2022aeneas]] and Verus [71 [#bib-lattuada2023verus]] trust rustc, LLVM and a model of Rust’s semantics, so we verify the compiler’s output directly. Ironclad Apps [57 [#bib-hawblitzel2014ironclad]] proved end-to-end security properties of applications at the assembly level, but for applications written in Dafny for verification.

 AI for formal verification. Language models have been extensively used to generate formal proofs over the last few years, in both mathematics [82 [#bib-polu2020gptf], 103 [#bib-yang2023leandojo], 62 [#bib-hubert2026olympiad], 96 [#bib-tsoukalas2026advancing]] and software verification. Some efforts in the latter category generate proof annotations in verification-aware languages like Dafny, Verus and F⋆^\star⋆ [100 [#bib-wu2024lemur], 92 [#bib-sun2024clover], 102 [#bib-yang2025autoverus], 39 [#bib-chakraborty2025fstar]]. Others focus on automatically discharging proof obligations for interactive provers [94 [#bib-thakur2024copra], 106 [#bib-zhang2024selene], 53 [#bib-first2023baldur]].

 AI capabilities in this area are growing rapidly. In 2025, an agent completed a formalization of the strong prime number theorem in about 25,000 lines of Lean [75 [#bib-mathinc2025pnt]]. In September 2026, a multi-agent AI system formalized a proof of Fermat’s Last Theorem in about 13 million lines [9 [#bib-anthropic2026flt]]. In software verification, AI systems have been primarily evaluated on the verification of small individual programs [93 [#bib-thakur2026clever], 104 [#bib-ye2026verina]], though there is early work on repository-level verification [101 [#bib-xin2026verisoftbench]].

 A number of recent efforts, carried out concurrently with this effort, have highlighted the ability of AI to scale up verification to cover compiled machine code. This includes MachCSL, which verifies the xv6 OS kernel against RISC-V privileged hardware semantics [65 [#bib-kaashoek2026machcsl]], verification of open-source fuzz targets compiled to RISC-V [11 [#bib-anthropic2026verification]], and Zellic’s work on verifying glibc’s startup on x86 [105 [#bib-zellic2026x86]]. These efforts largely target generic properties, such as crash freedom, or functional correctness. By contrast, our work shows that verifying security of a complex deployed system requires sophisticated specifications, which are substantial engineering projects in their own right. This is why AlphaProof Code supports AI-assisted specification discovery as well as autonomous proof.

 9 Discussion

 We have introduced AlphaProof Code, a framework in which humans and AI agents collaborate on the formal specification and verification of software, and used it to prove the security of libsignal, the Signal protocol’s reference implementation, at the machine-code level. Our security proofs cover both the symbolic and the computational models. While there is a long line of prior work on formal verification of cryptographic protocols [32 [#bib-blanchet2016proverif], 76 [#bib-meier2013tamarin], 17 [#bib-barthe2011easycrypt], 43 [#bib-cohngordon2020signal], 28 [#bib-bhargavan2024pqxdh], 40 [#bib-cheval2026doubleratchet], 27 [#bib-bhargavan2013mitls], 67 [#bib-signalstar]], ours are the first proofs of protocol-level security properties for the unmodified code of a widely deployed implementation of a cryptographic protocol.

 Our results can be extended in at least three directions. First, the theorems can be strengthened. Our computational theorem is classical, and our ratchet theorems let the attacker choose SPQR’s keys, so neither proves the post-quantum healing that SPQR is designed to provide. The model choices of §5.2.5 [#sec-limitations] and §5.1.6 [#sec-sym-limitations], such as a single session per handshake and fixed message lengths, can be relaxed. Second, the trusted base can be reduced. The two output functions and a few library routines are trusted contracts; verifying them would move the trust boundary down to the system calls. The low-level cryptographic routines have idealized contracts, which could be connected to verified implementations [108 [#bib-zinzindohoue2017hacl], 44 [#bib-libcrux]]. Third, the scope can be expanded beyond one-to-one sessions, for example, to Signal’s group messaging, sealed sender and server-side protocols.

 More generally, our work is one of several recent efforts [11 [#bib-anthropic2026verification], 65 [#bib-kaashoek2026machcsl]] that point to a shift in the level of abstraction at which code is verified. The attraction of machine-code-level verification is that one needs to trust less—there is no need to model the semantics of high-level languages like Rust and C++ or assume that the compiler is correct. However, proofs at this level are long; when humans wrote almost all formal proofs, they were impractical beyond restricted domains. AI changes this equation by drastically reducing the labor cost of formal proof. That said, verifying at the source level remains a worthwhile way to reduce the compute cost of proof. For example, a substantial fraction of our proofs concerns registers, stack frames and memory layout, which source-level abstractions would hide. Extending our methods to higher-level languages would lower this cost, at the price of trusting the compiler and the language semantics, or of verifying them as well [73 [#bib-leroy2009compcert], 87 [#bib-sewell2013translation]].

 One lesson is that while proofs are now much cheaper, specifications remain hard. The specification of a nontrivial software artifact is itself a nontrivial software artifact. Developing the software engineering discipline around formal specification is likely to become a fruitful area of research—conceptual frameworks, interfaces, and workflows through which humans and agents develop and validate a formal definition of what correctness and security mean. The process of §7 [#sec-ai], in which agents draft, review and test specifications under human direction, is a glimpse of how humans and AI might share this work. Like many of the most important problems in software engineering, this is a socio-technical challenge. Specifications of widely deployed systems need to be agreed upon by the communities that maintain, analyze and rely on them. Complex specifications, such as ours, will take many rounds of open review and refinement to settle. We are open-sourcing all Lean specifications and proofs, to facilitate such a process.

 In the longer run, we expect AI-driven specification and verification to be tightly coupled with AI-driven coding. Today, verification happens after the code is written, if at all. Once every piece of code comes with a formal specification, and AI proofs are cheap, every change to the code can be verified before it is merged. AlphaProof Code extends naturally to this setting. It treats the specification as a living artifact, revised through reviewed amendments when proofs reveal that it is wrong (§2.3 [#sec-proof]); code changes can be handled the same way, with groups of prover agents repairing the proofs that are affected when the code and (in many cases) the specification change. We are keen to use this workflow to verify new releases of libsignal before they ship, and also more broadly, as well. The coupling of code generation, specification and verification through AI could at last make machine-checked proofs of correctness and security routine for critical systems.

 A The Signal Protocol in Detail

 This appendix gives the details of PQXDH and the Triple Ratchet that §3 [#sec-signal] omits (Figure 8 [#fig-signal-detail]).

 [paper/figures/fig-signal-detail.svg]

Figure 8. The Signal protocol as implemented by libsignal, in detail. Left: Bob uploads his prekey bundle to the server; Alice fetches it, runs PQXDH, and sends a prekey message that carries the handshake values together with her first encrypted message. She keeps sending prekey messages until Bob replies; after that, the parties exchange ordinary Triple Ratchet messages. Right, simplified: the handshake secret SK\mathit{SK}SK yields a root key, a chain key and an SPQR key. Each reply with a new ratchet key mixes a fresh Diffie-Hellman output into the root key; each message advances the symmetric chain; and each message key is mixed with a key from SPQR before it is used to encrypt and authenticate the message.

 PQXDH. Each party has a long-term X25519 identity key IK\mathit{IK}IK. Bob uploads to the server a prekey bundle: a signed prekey SPKB\mathit{SPK}_BSPKB​ and a Kyber1024 prekey PQPKB\mathit{PQPK}_BPQPKB​ (single-use, or a reusable last-resort key), both signed with IKB\mathit{IK}_BIKB​ (XEdDSA), and optionally single-use one-time prekeys OPKB\mathit{OPK}_BOPKB​. Alice fetches the bundle, verifies the signatures, generates an ephemeral key EKA\mathit{EK}_AEKA​ and computes DH1=DH(IKA,SPKB)\mathit{DH}_1=\mathsf{DH}(\mathit{IK}_A,\mathit{SPK}_B)DH1​=DH(IKA​,SPKB​), DH2=DH(EKA,IKB)\mathit{DH}_2=\mathsf{DH}(\mathit{EK}_A,\mathit{IK}_B)DH2​=DH(EKA​,IKB​), DH3=DH(EKA,SPKB)\mathit{DH}_3=\mathsf{DH}(\mathit{EK}_A,\mathit{SPK}_B)DH3​=DH(EKA​,SPKB​) and, if a one-time prekey was used, DH4=DH(EKA,OPKB)\mathit{DH}_4=\mathsf{DH}(\mathit{EK}_A,\mathit{OPK}_B)DH4​=DH(EKA​,OPKB​). She encapsulates a secret ss\mathit{ss}ss to PQPKB\mathit{PQPK}_BPQPKB​, obtaining a ciphertext ct\mathit{ct}ct, and derives the session secret SK\mathit{SK}SK with HKDF from the DH values and ss\mathit{ss}ss. The DH values implicitly authenticate the parties, since only the holders of the identity keys can compute them, and ss\mathit{ss}ss protects SK\mathit{SK}SK against an adversary who records the handshake and later breaks Diffie-Hellman. Her first message, a PreKeySignalMessage, carries IKA\mathit{IK}_AIKA​, EKA\mathit{EK}_AEKA​, ct\mathit{ct}ct and the prekey identifiers together with her first encrypted message. Bob recomputes SK\mathit{SK}SK and deletes the one-time prekey.

 The Triple Ratchet. SK\mathit{SK}SK is split into a root key, a chain key and a seed for SPQR. In the Double Ratchet [80 [#bib-dr-spec]], the symmetric ratchet derives each message key and the next chain key from the current chain key with HMAC-SHA256, so old keys can be deleted (forward secrecy). Each message carries the sender’s ratchet public key, and a party that sees a new one mixes a fresh DH output into the root key, so an adversary who has compromised a party’s state loses access after the next round trip, provided it does not interfere with that round trip (post-compromise security). This healing holds only against classical adversaries, so the Sparse Post-Quantum Ratchet (SPQR) [89 [#bib-spqr]] runs an ML-KEM-768 exchange alongside, with keys and ciphertexts split into chunks carried in ordinary messages; each completed exchange yields an epoch key from which SPQR derives a key per message. The key that protects a message is derived from both the Double Ratchet’s and SPQR’s message keys. The message is encrypted with AES-256-CBC and authenticated with HMAC-SHA256 truncated to 8 bytes, over both identity keys and the protobuf-encoded SignalMessage, which carries the sender’s ratchet key, counters for out-of-order delivery, the SPQR chunk and the ciphertext. The receiver keeps a bounded number of keys of skipped messages.

 B Symbolic Proofs

 This appendix completes §5.1 [#sec-sym]: the composed model as a transition system (§B.1 [#app-sym-model]), the hardware events (§B.2 [#app-sym-events]), the concretization function (§B.3 [#app-sym-gamma]), explanations (§B.4 [#app-sym-explain]), the collision clause (§B.5 [#app-sym-collision]), the security statement and the scope of the binary theorem (§B.6 [#app-sym-sec], §B.7 [#app-sym-scope]), and the preservation proof (§B.8 [#app-sym-pres]).

 B.1 The Composed Model

 Each of the four libsignal entry points corresponds to one atomic transition in DyLean’s Traceful [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/Trace/Monad.lean#L9] monad (so the DH and SPQR ratchets execute atomically inside messageDecrypt [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___messageDecrypt], never exposing a half-ratcheted state). Transitions read from and append to a single global append-only trace (ExecTrace [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/Trace/Basic.lean#L309]), recording fresh randomness (genRand [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/Actions/Random.lean#L202]), network transmissions (sendMessage [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/Actions/Network.lean#L22]), immutable session-state snapshots referenced by trace handles (storeLocalState [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/Actions/PersistentLocalState.lean#L60]), and protocol events (logEvent [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/Actions/ProtocolEvent.lean#L198]). Compromises, exposures and breaks are protocol events (such as CompromisedIK [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHEvent___CompromisedIK], ExposedRK [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TREvent___ExposedRK] and BrokenDH [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHEvent___BrokenDH]); a compromise or exposure then sends the leaked secret on the network. Table 3 [#tab-composed-transitions] summarizes all 23 transitions of the composed state machine (ReachabilityConfig [https://github.com/BobDyLean/dylean/blob/74b1b2db1f188ba97d84d87637a6d2709a4e7508/DY/Trace/Reachability.lean#L11]), which store long-term and prekey secrets (PQXDHState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHState]) as well as session snapshots (TRState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TRState]) on the trace; any transition may fire at any time on attacker-chosen arguments whose guards hold.

Network, PKI, and prekey upload

NetworkAttacker sends any term derivable from its current knowledge onto the network.

PQXDH.registerIdentity [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___registerIdentity]Draws a fresh identity secret (once per party), logs honest PKI registration, sends public and verification keys, and stores PQXDHState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHState].

PQXDH.registerCorruptIdentity [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___registerCorruptIdentity]Reads an attacker-known secret (once per party) and logs dishonest PKI registration and CompromisedIK [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHEvent___CompromisedIK]; stores no local state.

PQXDH.genSignedPrekey [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___genSignedPrekey]Reads identity key from PQXDHState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHState], draws a signed prekey, logs upload, stores PQXDHState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHState], and sends public key and signature.

PQXDH.genPQPrekey [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___genPQPrekey]Reads identity key from PQXDHState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHState], draws a Kyber1024 prekey, logs upload, stores PQXDHState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHState], and sends public key and signature.

PQXDH.genOneTimePrekey [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___genOneTimePrekey]Draws an unsigned one-time prekey, logs upload, stores PQXDHState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHState], and sends the public key.

Session start and Triple Ratchet messaging

aliceStartVerifies Bob’s bundle (process_prekey_bundle), derives PQXDH keys, sends base key and KEM ciphertext, logs AliceDone [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHEvent___AliceDone], and stores initial TRState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TRState].

bobStartAtomically runs PQXDH and decrypts the opening message (message_decrypt_prekey); marks any one-time prekey used, logs BobDone [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.PQXDH/#DY___Libsignal___PQXDH___PQXDHEvent___BobDone] and skipped seeds, and stores TRState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TRState].

TripleRatchet.messageEncrypt [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___messageEncrypt]Loads current TRState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TRState], draws plaintext and SPQR coins, publishes SPQR key, advances sender chain, stores updated TRState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TRState], and sends wire message.

TripleRatchet.messageEncryptAbort [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___messageEncryptAbort]Loads current TRState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TRState], draws a fresh plaintext on the trace, and aborts without updating state or sending a message.

TripleRatchet.messageDecrypt [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___messageDecrypt]Loads current TRState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TRState] (DH-ratcheting on a new key), publishes SPQR key, verifies and decrypts message, logs skipped seeds and Received [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TREvent___Received], and updates TRState [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Model.TripleRatchet/#DY___Libsignal___TripleRatchet___TRState].

Adversarial compromises and cryptographic breaks

 B.2 Hardware Events Lean: RunEvent [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Concrete.MachineEvents/#DY___Libsignal___HwEvents___RunEvent] Lean: EnvEvent [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Concrete.MachineEvents/#DY___Libsignal___HwEvents___EnvEvent]

 A hardware trace is a list of events (CliEvent [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Concrete.MachineEvents/#DY___Libsignal___HwEvents___CliEvent]s). A run event holds the party, the command, the run’s input, its coins, and its exit code with its standard output. The attacker may invert a key only after the matching break, and only a key that an honest party generated: a key of the setup, or one that a successful run drew from its coins. Table 4 [#tab-events] lists every event.

 Lean definition: CliEvent

 inductiveDY.Libsignal.HwEvents.CliEvent Symbolic.Specifications.Concrete.MachineEvents (L60–62) → [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Concrete.MachineEvents/#DY___Libsignal___HwEvents___CliEvent]

 inductive CliEvent where
 | run (e : RunEvent [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Concrete.MachineEvents/#DY___Libsignal___HwEvents___RunEvent])
 | env (e : EnvEvent [Tier-2___-definitions/Symbolic___Specifications___/Symbolic.Specifications.Concrete.MachineEvents/#DY___Libsignal___HwEvents___EnvEvent])

Table 4. The hardware-level events. Every run event also records the run’s party, its coins and its exit code; a run that does not exit 0 is matched by no model transition. The last column gives the model transition that matches each event (§5.1.4 [#sec-refinement]). The attacker observes the bundles, the wire messages and the leaked or inverted secrets, which match network messages of the model trace, but not the plaintexts or the coins.
